\ket{\xi}_{A_{2}\bar{r}\bar{A}}
of
\rho_{\bar{r}\bar{A}}
has uniform Schmidt coefficients across the bipartition

\bar{r}\,:\,(A_{2}\bar{A})
. In particular, we may choose a Schmidt decomposition of the form

\ket{\xi}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bar{d}}}\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{d}}\ket{j}_{\bar{r}}\otimes\ket{\chi_{j}}_{A_{2}\bar{A}},

(250)

where the vectors
\{\ket{\chi_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{\bar{d}}
are orthonormal in
\mathcal{H}_{A_{2}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\bar{A}}
.

Substituting (
250
) into the purification
\ket{\phi_{0}^{\prime}}
and using
Eq. (
249
), we obtain

R_{AE}\ket{\phi}\ket{0}_{E}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{d}}\ket{ij}_{r\bar{r}}\otimes\ket{i}_{A_{1}}\otimes\ket{\chi_{j}}_{A_{2}\bar{A}}\;+\;\mathcal{N}(\epsilon)\,R_{AE}\ket{\delta}.

(251)

By the same argument applied to the
A
-supported part of the aKL condition, there exists a unitary
R_{\bar{A}\bar{E}}
on
\bar{A}
and auxiliary
\bar{E}
, and
a purification
\ket{\xi^{\prime}}_{\bar{A}_{2}rA}
of the corresponding reduced state such that

R_{\bar{A}\bar{E}}\ket{\phi}\ket{0}_{\bar{E}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{d}}\ket{ij}_{r\bar{r}}\otimes\ket{j}_{\bar{A}_{1}}\otimes\ket{\chi^{\prime}_{i}}_{\bar{A}_{2}A}\;+\;\overline{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)\,R_{\bar{A}\bar{E}}\ket{\delta^{\prime}},

(252)

with
\overline{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)\leq\sqrt{\epsilon\,\|Y\|/d_{L}}
.

Comparing (
251
) and (
252
) (they describe the same state
\ket{\phi}\ket{0}_{E\bar{E}}
after adjoining with auxiliary systems) gives an averaged bound:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d_{L}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{d}}\Bigl\|\ket{i}_{A_{1}}\,R_{\bar{A}\bar{E}}\ket{\chi_{j}}\ket{0}_{\bar{E}}-\ket{j}_{\bar{A}_{1}}\,R_{AE}\ket{\chi^{\prime}_{i}}\ket{0}_{E}\Bigr\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\overline{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)\,\ket{\delta^{\prime}}\ket{0}_{E}-\mathcal{N}(\epsilon)\,\ket{\delta}\ket{0}_{\bar{E}}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bigl(\mathcal{N}(\epsilon)+\overline{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)\bigr)^{2}.

(253)

Hence there exists a fixed index
i_{0}\in\{1,\dots,d\}
such that

\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{d}}\Bigl\|\ket{i_{0}}_{A_{1}}\,R_{\bar{A}\bar{E}}\ket{\chi_{j}}\ket{0}_{\bar{E}}-\ket{j}_{\bar{A}_{1}}\,R_{AE}\ket{\chi^{\prime}_{i_{0}}}\ket{0}_{E}\Bigr\|^{2}\leq\bar{d}\,\bigl(\mathcal{N}(\epsilon)+\overline{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)\bigr)^{2}.

(254)

Taking the inner product of each term in (
254
) with
\bra{i_{0}}_{A_{1}}
produces a joint (unnormalized) state on
A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}
,

\ket{\tilde{\chi}}_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}}\;:=\;(\bra{i_{0}}_{A_{1}}\otimes I_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}})\,R_{AE}\ket{\chi^{\prime}_{i_{0}}}_{\bar{A}_{2}A}\ket{0}_{E}.

Let

\ket{\chi}_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}}:=\frac{\ket{\tilde{\chi}}_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}}}{\sqrt{\bra{\tilde{\chi}}\tilde{\chi}\rangle}}.

Then (
254
) implies the following bound

\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{d}}\left\lVert R_{\bar{A}\bar{E}}\ket{\chi_{j}}_{A_{2}\bar{A}}\ket{0}_{\bar{E}}-\ket{j}_{\bar{A}_{1}}\otimes\ket{\chi}_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}}\right\rVert^{2}\leq\bar{d}\,\left(\mathcal{N}(\epsilon)+\overline{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)\right)^{2}.

(255)

Substituting (
255
) back into (
251
) gives the desired approximate factorization of the code basis.
Equivalently,

\displaystyle\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{d}}\Bigl\|\ket{\widetilde{ij}}\ket{0}_{E\bar{E}}-(R_{AE}^{\dagger}\otimes R_{\bar{A}\bar{E}}^{\dagger})\bigl(\ket{i}_{A_{1}}\otimes\ket{j}_{\bar{A}_{1}}\otimes\ket{\chi}_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}}\bigr)\Bigr\|^{2}}

(256)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{d_{L}}\left\lVert R_{AE}\otimes R_{\bar{A}\bar{E}}\ket{\phi}\ket{0}_{E\bar{E}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{d}}\ket{ij}_{r\bar{r}}\otimes\ket{ij}_{A_{1}\bar{A}_{1}}\otimes\ket{\chi}_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}}\right\rVert

(257)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{d}}\left\lVert R_{\bar{A}\bar{E}}\ket{\chi_{j}}\ket{0}_{\bar{E}}-\ket{j}_{\bar{A}_{1}}\otimes\ket{\chi}_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}}\right\rVert^{2}}+\sqrt{d_{L}}\,\mathcal{N}(\epsilon)

(258)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{d_{L}}\,\bigl(\mathcal{N}(\epsilon)+\overline{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)\bigr)

(259)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{\epsilon}\,\bigl(\sqrt{\|Y\|}+\sqrt{\|\bar{Y}\|}\bigr).

(260)

where in the last step we used the bounds on
\mathcal{N}(\epsilon)
and
\overline{\mathcal{N}}(\epsilon)
established earlier.

Now define, for each logical basis label
k\in\{1,\dots,d_{L}\}
,

\ket{t_{k}}\;:=(R_{AE}^{\dagger}\otimes R_{\bar{A}\bar{E}}^{\dagger})\;\ket{k}_{A_{1}\bar{A}_{1}}\otimes\ket{\chi}_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}}.

(261)

The vectors
\{\ket{t_{k}}\}_{k=1}^{d_{L}}
are orthonormal and span a subspace
\mathcal{H}_{T}\subset\mathcal{H}_{P^{\prime}}:=\mathcal{H}_{AE}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\bar{A}\bar{E}}

isomorphic to the unskewed (exact subsystem complementary) code subspace.
Extend
\{\ket{t_{k}}\}
to an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{P^{\prime}}
, and denote the orthogonal complement of
\mathcal{H}_{T}
by

\mathcal{H}_{T^{\perp}}
.

Define a unitary
U
on
\mathcal{H}_{P^{\prime}}
by specifying its action on
\mathcal{H}_{T}
and
\mathcal{H}_{T^{\perp}}
:

U\;:=\;\Bigl(\ket{0}_{E\bar{E}}\otimes\sum_{k=1}^{d_{L}}\ket{\tilde{k}}\!\bra{t_{k}}\Bigr)\;\oplus\;I_{\mathcal{H}_{T^{\perp}}}.

(262)

(Here
\{\ket{\tilde{k}}\}
is the corresponding orthonormal basis of the code
\mathcal{C}
, and the direct sum is with respect to

\mathcal{H}_{A\bar{A}}=\mathcal{H}_{T}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{T^{\perp}}
.)

We next bound
\|U-I\|
on
\mathcal{H}_{T}
. Let
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{P^{\prime}}
be arbitrary with expansion

\ket{\psi}=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{L}}\alpha_{i}\ket{t_{i}}+\alpha_{\perp}\ket{t^{\perp}}
, where
\ket{t^{\perp}}\in\mathcal{H}_{T^{\perp}}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{d_{L}}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}\leq 1
. Then

\displaystyle\|(U-I)\ket{\psi}\|

\displaystyle=\Bigl\|\sum_{i=1}^{d_{L}}\alpha_{i}\bigl(\ket{\tilde{i}}\ket{0}-\ket{t_{i}}\bigr)\Bigr\|\leq\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d_{L}}\|\ket{\tilde{i}}\ket{0}-\ket{t_{i}}\|^{2}}

(263)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\epsilon}\,\bigl(\sqrt{\|Y\|}+\sqrt{\|\bar{Y}\|}\bigr),

(264)

where we used the bound in Eq. (
260
) obtained previously.

Eq. (
264
) implies the spectral-norm (denoted as
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{2}
) bound

\|U-I\|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{\epsilon}\,\bigl(\sqrt{\|Y\|}+\sqrt{\|\bar{Y}\|}\bigr).

(265)

Finally, using the standard estimate
\|\log U\|_{2}\leq\frac{\pi}{2}\|U-I\|_{2}
for unitaries with spectrum in the principal branch,
together with (
265
), we conclude that there exists a Hermitian operator
W
on
\mathcal{H}_{A\bar{A}}
and a parameter
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
such that

U=e^{i\epsilon^{\prime}W}
with

\epsilon^{\prime}\,\|W\|_{2}=\|\log U\|_{2}\leq\pi\,\sqrt{\epsilon}\,\bigl(\sqrt{\|Y\|}+\sqrt{\|\bar{Y}\|}\bigr),

Using the norm
|Y|
and
|\bar{Y}|
, defined as

\displaystyle|Y|:=\sup_{\|X\|\leq 1}\max_{i,j}|Y_{ij}(X)|,\qquad|\bar{Y}|:=\sup_{\|X\|\leq 1}\max_{i,j}|\bar{Y}_{ij}(X)|,

(266)

we obtain

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}\,\|W\|_{2}\leq\pi\,\sqrt{\epsilon\,d_{L}}\,\bigl(\sqrt{d_{A}|Y|}+\sqrt{d_{\bar{A}}|\bar{Y}|}\bigr).

(267)

Since
U-I
vanishes on
\mathcal{H}_{T^{\perp}}
by definition, this leads to a bound in Frobenius norm:

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}\,\|W\|_{F}\leq\pi\,\sqrt{\epsilon}\,d_{L}\,\bigl(\sqrt{d_{A}|Y|}+\sqrt{d_{\bar{A}}|\bar{Y}|}\bigr)

(268)

We now prove the reverse implication (2)
\Rightarrow
(1).

In what follows, we incorporate the ancillas into the corresponding physical subsystems, i.e. we replace
A\leftarrow AE
and
\bar{A}\leftarrow\bar{A}\bar{E}
, and similarly for the codewords
\ket{\tilde{i}}_{P}\leftarrow\ket{i}_{P}\ket{0}_{E\bar{E}}
.

Assume the approximate recovery form (
36
), and write each codeword as

\ket{\tilde{i}}=e^{i\epsilon^{\prime}W}\ket{t_{i}},

(269)

where

\ket{t_{i}}:=(R_{A}^{\dagger}\otimes R_{\bar{A}}^{\dagger})\Bigl(\ket{i_{L}}_{A_{1}\bar{A}_{1}}\otimes\ket{\chi}_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}}\Bigr).

(270)

The family
\{\ket{t_{i}}\}
therefore consists of codewords of an unskewed subsystem complementary code.
Equivalently, there exists an isometry
V_{t}:\mathcal{H}_{L}\to\mathcal{H}_{P}
such that

\ket{t_{i}}=V_{t}\ket{i_{L}}\qquad(\forall\,i).

(271)

For an exact subsystem complementary code, there is a linear map

\mathcal{E}_{\bar{A}}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\bar{A}})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}_{\bar{a}})
satisfying, for all
X_{\bar{A}}
,

\bra{t_{i}}(I_{A}\otimes X_{\bar{A}})\ket{t_{j}}=\bra{i_{L}}\bigl(I_{a}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{\bar{A}}(X_{\bar{A}})\bigr)\ket{j_{L}}.

(272)

Moreover,
\mathcal{E}_{\bar{A}}
is completely positive: the reduced state obtained from the Choi vector

\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\sum_{i}\ket{i}_{r\bar{r}}\ket{t_{i}}
is (up to normalization and the identification
\bar{r}\simeq\mathcal{L}_{\bar{a}}
)
the Choi operator
J(\mathcal{E}_{\bar{A}})
, hence positive semidefinite, which is equivalent to complete positivity.

It remains to compare matrix elements in the perturbed code
\{\ket{\tilde{i}}\}
to those in the exact code
\{\ket{t_{i}}\}
.
For any operator
O\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{P})
,

\displaystyle\Bigl|\bra{\tilde{i}}O\ket{\tilde{j}}-\bra{t_{i}}O\ket{t_{j}}\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\Bigl|\bra{t_{i}}\bigl(e^{-i\epsilon^{\prime}W}Oe^{i\epsilon^{\prime}W}-O\bigr)\ket{t_{j}}\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\|e^{-i\epsilon^{\prime}W}Oe^{i\epsilon^{\prime}W}-O\bigr\|_{2}.

(273)

Using the integral representation of the conjugation derivative,

e^{-i\epsilon^{\prime}W}Oe^{i\epsilon^{\prime}W}-O=\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}ds\,\frac{d}{ds}\Bigl(e^{-isW}Oe^{isW}\Bigr)=\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}ds\,\bigl(-i\bigr)\,e^{-isW}[W,O]e^{isW},

and unitary invariance of the Hilbert–Schmidt norm, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigl\|e^{-i\epsilon^{\prime}W}Oe^{i\epsilon^{\prime}W}-O\bigr\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{\prime}}ds\,\|[W,O]\|_{2}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\,\|[W,O]\|_{2}\leq 2\epsilon^{\prime}\,\|W\|_{2}\,\|O\|_{2},

(274)

where the last inequality uses
\|AB\|_{2}\leq\|A\|_{2}\|B\|_{2}
and the triangle inequality.

In particular, for
O=I_{A}\otimes X_{\bar{A}}
with
\|X_{\bar{A}}\|_{2}=1
,
combining (
272
) with (
273
)–(
274
) yields

\displaystyle\epsilon\,|Y_{ij}(X_{\bar{A}})|

\displaystyle=\Bigl|\bra{\tilde{i}}(I_{A}\otimes X_{\bar{A}})\ket{\tilde{j}}-\bra{i_{L}}\bigl(I_{a}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{\bar{A}}(X_{\bar{A}})\bigr)\ket{j_{L}}\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\Bigl|\bra{\tilde{i}}(I_{A}\otimes X_{\bar{A}})\ket{\tilde{j}}-\bra{t_{i}}(I_{A}\otimes X_{\bar{A}})\ket{t_{j}}\Bigr|\leq 2\epsilon^{\prime}\,\|W\|_{2}.

(275)

This is exactly the desired aKL bound for
\bar{A}
-supported operators (and the
A
-supported case is identical), completing the proof.
∎

A.7
Theorem
4.5

We generalize the PA entropy calculation to the case where the resource state
\ket{\chi}
has a non-flat entanglement spectrum.
The proof follows the same steps as the flat-spectrum calculation in Appendix (
A.2
), with the only difference that the reduced state
\chi_{A_{2}}
is no longer proportional to the identity. We therefore indicate only the required modifications to the Weingarten (WG) evaluation and to the spectral
s
-integrals. All other steps (the perturbative relative-entropy expansion (
61
), the Haar averaging over
U,V
on
A_{1}
and
\bar{A}_{1}
, and the diagrammatic reduction to
W
–
\mathscr{R}
building blocks) are unchanged.

In this section, we focus on the case where
|\bar{A}_{1}|
is empty, generalizing Theorem
4.2
.
Write the Schmidt decomposition of the resource state as

\ket{\chi}_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}}=\sum_{p}\sqrt{\mu_{p}}\,\ket{p}_{A_{2}}\ket{p}_{\bar{A}_{2}},\qquad\mu_{p}>0,\ \ \sum_{p}\mu_{p}=1,

(276)

so that the reduced state
\chi_{A_{2}}
is diagonal with possible degeneracies,

\chi_{A_{2}}=\sum_{m}\mu_{m}\,\Pi_{m}\equiv\bigoplus_{m}\mu_{m}\,I_{m},

(277)

where
\Pi_{m}
acting on
A_{2}
, is the projector onto the eigenspace of eigenvalue
\mu_{m}
, and
I_{m}
denotes the identity on that
eigenspace.

In the unperturbed limit, the recovered boundary state of an exact code factorizes, so

\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}A_{2}}=\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}\otimes\chi_{A_{2}}
, and the resolvent appearing in

D_{\ln}(\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}A_{2}})
is

\Gamma_{A_{1}A_{2}}(s):=(\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}\otimes\chi_{A_{2}}+sI)^{-1}=\sum_{m}\Gamma_{m}(s)\otimes\Pi_{m},\qquad\Gamma_{m}(s):=(\mu_{m}\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}+sI)^{-1}.

(278)

For the boundary-WG diagrams, every occurrence of the flat-spectrum resolvent

\Gamma(s)=(d_{\chi}^{-1}\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}+sI)^{-1}
is now replaced according to the the block
decomposition (
278
). For example, any boundary term containing

\Tr(\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}\Gamma(s))\,\Tr(\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}\Gamma(s))
is replaced by

\displaystyle\Tr(\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}\Gamma_{A_{1}A_{2}}(s))\,\Tr(\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}\Gamma_{A_{1}A_{2}}(s))\rightarrow\Tr(\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}\Gamma_{m}(s))\,\Tr(\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}\Gamma_{n}(s)).

(279)

Similar block decomposition also occurs to operators whose support has overlap with
A_{2}
. To make this structure explicit, we adopt the convention that for any operator
X
on
MA_{2}
we write its

\chi_{A_{2}}
-eigenbasis block components as

X_{mn}:=(I_{M}\otimes\Pi_{m})\,X\,(I_{M}\otimes\Pi_{n}),

(280)

where
M
denotes any subregions of
A_{1}\bar{A}
. We have
X=\sum_{m,n}X_{mn}
and each
X_{mn}
is a linear map

\mathcal{H}_{M}\otimes\mathrm{Ran}(\Pi_{n})\to\mathcal{H}_{M}\otimes\mathrm{Ran}(\Pi_{m})
. As a result, each boundary WG contraction splits into a sum over block labels. For example, the flat-spectrum combination

\int ds\,\mathscr{R}_{1}(s)\,\mathcal{W}_{2}
is replaced by

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\mathscr{R}_{1}(s)\,\mathcal{W}_{2}\

\displaystyle\rightarrow\ \sum_{m,n}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\Tr\!\big(\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}\Gamma_{m}(s)\big)\,\Tr\!\big(\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}\Gamma_{n}(s)\big)\,\times

(281)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\Tr\!\Big[\Tr_{\bar{A}}\!\big((W_{R}\chi)_{mn}\big)\,\Tr_{\bar{A}}\!\big((W_{R}\chi)_{nm}\big)\Big],

(282)

where
(W_{R}\chi)_{mn}
denotes the
(m,n)
block defined in (
280
). Each
block
(\cdot)_{mn}
can be viewed as a linear map

\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}\bar{A}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A_{2}^{n}}\to\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}\bar{A}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A_{2}^{m}}
.

On the bulk side, the WG evaluation is unchanged: all bulk diagrams trace out
A_{2}
(as in the flat case, e.g. the analogues of
W_{7}
and
W_{8}
), so no
A_{2}
-block labels survive in the resolvent. Equivalently, the bulk relative entropy still depends on
A_{1}
only through the same operator

\Gamma(s)=(d_{\chi}^{-1}\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}+sI)^{-1},

(283)

while the dependence on
\chi
enters only through the
A_{2}
-traced contractions of
W_{R}\chi

We now express the resulting block-summed answer in a compact operator form. Define the Hermitian operators

J:=d_{\chi}\,\Tr_{\bar{A}_{2}}\!\Big(\{W_{R},\ket{\chi}\bra{\chi}\}\Big),\qquad D:=id_{\chi}\,\Tr_{\bar{A}_{2}}\!\Big([W_{R},\ket{\chi}\bra{\chi}]\Big),

(284)

same as the definitions in (
96
) in the flat-spectrum case. In the eigenbasis of

\chi_{A_{2}}
, we decompose
J
and
D
into block components by inserting the projectors
\Pi_{m}
on
A_{2}
:

J_{mn}:=(I_{A_{1}}\otimes\Pi_{m})\,J\,(I_{A_{1}}\otimes\Pi_{n}),\qquad D_{mn}:=(I_{A_{1}}\otimes\Pi_{m})\,D\,(I_{A_{1}}\otimes\Pi_{n}),

(285)

so that
J=\sum_{m,n}J_{mn}
and
D=\sum_{m,n}D_{mn}
,
matching the block-index structure produced by resolving every
A_{2}
wire as explained above.

After performing the Haar average over
U
on
A_{1}
, the same Weingarten rules as in Appendix (
A.2
)
again separate the answer into a product of (i) spectral functions coming from the
s
-integrals involving

\Gamma_{m}(s)
and
\Gamma_{n}(s)
, and (ii) purely algebraic contractions of
W_{R}
and
\chi
, which
can be written in terms of the blocks
J_{mn}
and
D_{mn}
. We record the resulting replacements in the same
order as the flat-spectrum decomposition (
30
), starting with the
J
-sector.

Concretely, the flat contribution
c_{1}f_{1}(\lambda)
is promoted to a block-sum

c_{1}f_{1}(\lambda)\ \longrightarrow\ \sum_{m,n}c_{1}^{mn}\,f_{1}^{mn}(\lambda),

(286)

where
c_{1}^{mn}
is obtained from the
J
-dependent trace combinations in (
99
) by resolving every

A_{2}
-contraction into block components.

Equivalently, one applies the rule

J\ \mapsto\ J_{mn},\qquad J^{2}\ \mapsto\ J_{mn}J_{nm},\qquad\Tr_{A_{1}}(J)\ \mapsto\ \Tr_{A_{1}}(J_{mn}),

and similarly for the remaining traced quantities.
In particular, each occurrence of
\Tr(J^{2})
is replaced by
\Tr(J_{mn}J_{nm})
, and each occurrence of

\Tr(\Tr_{M}(J)^{2})
is replaced, in the same manner, by

\Tr\!\big(\Tr_{M}(J_{mn})\,\Tr_{M}(J_{nm})\big)
, where M is any subsystem of
A\bar{A}

Hence, we have:

\displaystyle c_{1}^{mn}(J):=\frac{d}{2d_{\chi}\left(d^{2}-1\right)}\Bigg[

\displaystyle\Tr_{A_{1}A_{2}}\!\left(J_{mn}J_{nm}\right)-\frac{1}{d}\,\Tr_{A_{2}}\!\left(\Tr_{A_{1}}(J_{mn})\,\Tr_{A_{1}}(J_{nm})\right)

(287)

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta_{mn}}{d_{\chi}}\,\Tr_{A_{1}}\!\left(\Tr_{A_{2}}(J_{mm})^{2}\right)+\frac{\delta_{mn}}{d\,d_{\chi}}\,\Tr_{A_{1}A_{2}}\!\left(J_{mm}\right)^{2}\Bigg].

The terms in
c_{1}
that originate from tracing out
A_{2}
remain diagonal in the block labels, since a complete trace over
A_{2}
collapses
\Pi_{m}\Pi_{n}=\delta_{mn}\Pi_{m}
. The corresponding spectral factor
f_{1}^{mn}(\lambda)
is obtained from the same linear combination of
s
-integrals that defines
f_{1}(\lambda)
in (
91
), with the boundary resolvents replaced as
\Gamma(s)\mapsto\Gamma_{m}(s)
and
\Gamma(s)\mapsto\Gamma_{n}(s)
. Writing

\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}|i\rangle\langle i|
, one convenient closed form is

f_{1}^{mn}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2d}\sum_{ij}\frac{(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})^{2}}{(\mu_{m}\lambda_{i}-\mu_{n}\lambda_{j})}\ln\frac{\mu_{m}\lambda_{i}}{\mu_{n}\lambda_{j}}.

(288)

with the continuous extension at
\mu_{m}\lambda_{i}=\mu_{n}\lambda_{j}
understood. When
\mu_{m}=\mu_{n}=1/d_{\chi}
this reduces to the flat-spectrum function
f_{1}(\lambda)
.

Next, the contribution that in the flat case recombined into
c_{2}f_{2}(\lambda)
is promoted to

c_{2}f_{2}(\lambda)\ \longrightarrow\ \sum_{m,n}c_{2}^{mn}\,f_{2}^{mn}(\lambda),

(289)

with coefficients expressed by the same
D
-trace combinations as in (
99
), but evaluated on the block products
D_{mn}D_{nm}
:

c_{2}^{mn}(D):=\frac{d}{2d_{\chi}(d^{2}-1)}\left[\Tr_{A_{1}A_{2}}\!\big(D_{mn}D_{nm}\big)-\frac{1}{d}\,\Tr_{A_{2}}\!\Big(\Tr_{A_{1}}(D_{mn})\,\Tr_{A_{1}}(D_{nm})\Big)\right].

(290)

The associated spectral function
f_{2}^{mn}(\lambda)
is obtained from the same linear combination of
s
-integrals that defines
f_{2}(\lambda)
in (
91
), with the replacement
\Gamma(s)\mapsto\Gamma_{m}(s)
and

\Gamma(s)\mapsto\Gamma_{n}(s)
. In the eigenbasis of
\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}
, a convenient closed form is

f_{2}^{mn}(\lambda)=\sum_{i,j}\frac{1}{2d}\,\frac{(\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j})^{2}}{\mu_{m}\lambda_{i}-\mu_{n}\lambda_{j}}\,\ln\!\frac{\mu_{m}\lambda_{i}}{\mu_{n}\lambda_{j}}\;,

(291)

where again the continuous extension at
\mu_{m}\lambda_{i}=\mu_{n}\lambda_{j}
is understood, and the constant terms arise from the same normalization pieces as in the flat-spectrum evaluation.

Collecting these block-resolved contributions together with the similarly promoted constant term

c_{3}\to\sum_{m,n}c_{3}^{mn}
yields the stated structure

\langle S_{\mathrm{corr}}\rangle=\epsilon\,c_{0}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}\sum_{m,n}\Big(c_{1}^{mn}f_{1}^{mn}(\lambda)+c_{2}^{mn}f_{2}^{mn}(\lambda)+c_{3}^{mn}\Big),

where
c_{0}
arises solely from the term
-\Tr\!\big(\delta\chi\,\ln\chi\big)
in the general proto-area identity
Eq. (
58
). For a flat entanglement spectrum of
\chi_{A_{2}}
one has
\delta\chi=0
and this contribution
vanishes, whereas for a non-flat spectrum it is generically nonzero and produces an
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
correction
after Haar averaging.

The monotonicity statements follow from Appendix
C
after the block replacement. For each fixed pair
(m,n)
, we have shown in Appendix
C
that the functions
f^{mn}_{1}(\lambda)
and
f^{mn}_{2}(\lambda)
are each monotonically decreasing with bulk entanglement (even though their
s
-integral representations differ from those of
f_{1},f_{2}
). Since the corresponding coefficients
c_{i}^{mn}
are nonnegative by construction, the block-summed correction inherits these monotonicity properties.
∎

A.8
Theorem
4.6

We now extend Theorem (
4.3
) to a general resource state

\ket{\chi}_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}}
with non-flat entanglement spectrum. The derivation proceeds exactly as in the flat-spectrum pure-state analysis of Appendix (
A.4
), with the only change being the block-resolution procedure introduced in the mixed-state generalization of Appendix (
A.7
).

Specifically, every boundary and bulk trace diagram containing an
A_{2}
wire is modified by the same replacement rule:
\chi_{A_{2}}
is promoted to the direct sum of spectral projectors (see Eq. (
277
)), while the corresponding
A_{1}A_{2}
resolvents are replaced by their block-diagonal versions (see Eq. (
278
)). Accordingly, we record only the resulting promoted coefficients and spectral functions.

Define
J
and
D
exactly as in the flat-spectrum pure case,

J:=d_{\chi}\,\Tr_{\bar{A}_{2}}\!\left(\{W_{R},\ket{\chi}\bra{\chi}\}\right),\qquad D:=id_{\chi}\,\Tr_{\bar{A}_{2}}\!\left([W_{R},\ket{\chi}\bra{\chi}]\right),

(292)

now regarded as operators acting on
A_{1}\bar{A}_{1}A_{2}
. Decomposing
A_{2}
into the eigenspaces of
\chi_{A_{2}}
induces corresponding block components
J_{mn}
and
D_{mn}
, viewed as maps from the
n
-th eigenspace of
\chi_{A_{2}}
to the
m
-th eigenspace.

Upon carrying out the same independent local Haar averages over
U
on
A_{1}
and
V
on
\bar{A}_{1}
, the Weingarten calculus produces the same diagrammatic structure as in Appendix (
A.4
), with the only change that each flat-spectrum coefficient is promoted to a double sum over block labels
(m,n)
. Accordingly, the flat-spectrum decomposition in Eq. (
178
) is replaced by

\displaystyle\big\langle S_{\mathrm{corr}}\big\rangle=

\displaystyle\epsilon k_{0}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}\sum_{m,n}\bigg[k_{1}^{mn}f^{mn}_{1}(\lambda)+k_{2}^{mn}f^{mn}_{2}(\lambda)+k_{3}^{mn}\!\left(f^{mn}_{3}(\lambda)-\frac{1}{d^{2}}\Bigl(1+\frac{d}{\bar{d}}\Bigr)f_{2}^{mn}(\lambda)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{1}{d^{2}}f_{1}^{mn}(\lambda)\right)+k_{4}^{mn}\!\left(f^{mn}_{3}(\lambda)-\frac{1}{d\bar{d}}f_{1}^{mn}(\lambda)\right)+k_{5}^{mn}\!\left(f_{2}^{mn}(\lambda)-f_{1}^{mn}(\lambda)\right)+k_{6}^{mn}\bigg],

(293)

where
\dim\mathcal{L}_{a}=d
and
\dim\mathcal{L}_{\bar{a}}=\bar{d}
as before, and
f_{3}^{mn}
is defined as:

f_{3}^{mn}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2d}\sum_{i,j}\frac{\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j}}{\left(\mu_{m}\lambda_{i}-\mu_{n}\lambda_{j}\right)}\ln\left(\frac{\mu_{m}\lambda_{i}}{\mu_{n}\lambda_{j}}\right).

(294)

Each coefficient
k_{\ell}^{mn}
is obtained from its flat-spectrum counterpart
k_{\ell}
by promoting
J\to J_{mn}
and
D\to D_{mn}
within the same trace polynomials. In particular, every occurrence of
\Tr(J^{2})
and
\Tr(D^{2})
is replaced by
\Tr(J_{mn}J_{nm})
and
\Tr(D_{mn}D_{nm})
, respectively, while every squared partial trace is replaced by the corresponding product of blockwise partial traces. For example,

\Tr\!\big(\Tr_{M}(J)^{2}\big)\ \mapsto\ \Tr\!\big(\Tr_{M}(J_{mn})\,\Tr_{M}(J_{nm})\big),

\Tr\!\big(\Tr_{M}(D)^{2}\big)\ \mapsto\ \Tr\!\big(\Tr_{M}(D_{mn})\,\Tr_{M}(D_{nm})\big),

for any subsystem
M
appearing in
k_{\ell}
.

The spectral functions
f_{\ell}^{mn}(\lambda)
are defined by evaluating the same
s
-integral combinations that define
f_{\ell}(\lambda)
, but with the
A_{1}A_{2}
resolvent factors on the two boundary legs replaced by
(\Gamma_{m}(s),\Gamma_{n}(s))
. Equivalently, after diagonalizing

\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\,|i\rangle\langle i|
,
they may be obtained from the flat-spectrum expressions by replacing

\lambda_{i}\mapsto\nu_{m}\lambda_{i}
and
\lambda_{j}\mapsto\nu_{n}\lambda_{j}

wherever these eigenvalues enter through the resolvents. In the special case
\nu_{m}=\nu_{n}=1/d_{\chi}
, these definitions reduce to the flat-spectrum ones. Their monotonicity then follows pointwise in
(m,n)
from the same integrand-level argument used in Appendix
C
.

Finally, because
\chi_{A_{2}}
is not maximally mixed, the proto-area identity acquires an additional first-order contribution,

\epsilon\,k_{0},

where
k_{0}
arises from the term
\Tr(\delta\chi\,\ln\chi)
in Eq. (
58
). Combining all block-resolved contributions yields the statement of the theorem, with

\langle S_{PA}\rangle=S(\chi)-\langle S_{\mathrm{corr}}\rangle

and
\langle S_{\mathrm{corr}}\rangle
given by Eq. (
293
).

For each fixed block pair
(m,n)
, the functions
f^{mn}_{1}(\lambda)
,
f^{mn}_{2}(\lambda)
, and
f^{mn}_{3}(\lambda)
are monotonically decreasing in
\lambda
by the same integrand-level argument as in Appendix
C
. Thus, in (
293
) the only potentially non-monotone structure is the difference
f^{mn}_{2}(\lambda)-f^{mn}_{1}(\lambda)
, which appears with coefficient
k^{mn}_{5}
. The typicality argument from the flat-spectrum pure case carries over directly: under the same Gaussian-ensemble model for the product-basis coefficients of
W_{R}
, each promoted coefficient
k^{mn}_{\ell}
concentrates around its term-counting estimate, and the coefficient sector multiplying
f^{mn}_{2}-f^{mn}_{1}
is typically suppressed relative to the dominant monotone sector. Since (
293
) is a finite sum over
(m,n)
, a union bound implies that the probability that any block produces a monotonicity-violating fluctuation remains exponentially small (up to a prefactor polynomial in
d_{\chi}
), exactly as in the flat-spectrum analysis. So in the large
d
and
\bar{d}
limit, we find that the
typical
PA entropy correction in the non-flat case
is dominated by the block-summed
f^{mn}_{3}
sector,

\big\langle S_{\mathrm{corr}}\big\rangle=\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}\sum_{m,n}\big(k^{mn}_{3}+k^{mn}_{4}\big)\,f^{mn}_{3}(\lambda)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{1}{d^{2}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{1}{\bar{d}^{2}}\right).

(295)

∎

Appendix B
Magic of the code

In this section, we compute the magic of the skewed code. In the main text, we have defined the Choi state of the skewed code to be

\ket{V^{(\epsilon)}}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\sum_{i=1}^{d_{L}}\ket{i}_{R}\otimes e^{i\epsilon W_{R}}\ket{i}_{A_{1}\bar{A}_{1}}\ket{\chi}_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}}.

(296)

The magic of the state is quantified using stabilizer Renyi entropy (SRE), defined as

\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}(\ket{V^{(\epsilon)}})=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\left(2^{-n}\sum_{s=1}^{4^{n}}|\bra{V^{(\epsilon)}}P_{s}\ket{V^{(\epsilon)}}|^{2\alpha}\right),

(297)

where
P_{s}
’s are the Pauli string operators on
R\cup A_{1}\cup\bar{A}_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup\bar{A}_{2}
.
We decompose the Pauli string operator
P_{s}
into tensor product of four parts,
P_{a},P_{b},P_{c},P_{d}
, supported on
R,A_{1}\bar{A}_{1},A_{2},
and
\bar{A}_{2}
respectively. Up to order
\epsilon^{2}
, expectation value of the Pauli string is as:

\displaystyle|\langle V^{(\epsilon)}|P_{a}\otimes P_{b}\otimes P_{c}\otimes P_{d}|V^{(\epsilon)}\rangle|

(298)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d_{L}d_{\chi}}\left[\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height]{theory_draft_images/SRE_diagrams/PaPbPcPd.png}}}+i\epsilon\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height]{theory_draft_images/SRE_diagrams/PsW_R.png}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height]{theory_draft_images/SRE_diagrams/W_RPs.png}}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\hskip 28.45274pt-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height]{theory_draft_images/SRE_diagrams/PsW_RW_R.png}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height]{theory_draft_images/SRE_diagrams/W_RW_RPs.png}}}-2\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height]{theory_draft_images/SRE_diagrams/W_RPsW_R.png}}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\equiv\delta_{ab}\delta_{cd}+\epsilon f_{abcd}-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}g_{abcd}+O(\epsilon^{3})

where
f_{abcd}
and
g_{abcd}
are defined in the equation.

Using this expansion, we derive the SRE up to second order of
\epsilon
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\!\left(\ket{V^{(\epsilon)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\!\left[\frac{1}{(d_{L}d_{\chi})^{2}}\sum_{abcd}\left|\delta_{ab}\delta_{cd}+\epsilon f_{abcd}-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}g_{abcd}\right|^{2\alpha}\right]+O(\epsilon^{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\!\left[\frac{1}{(d_{L}d_{\chi})^{2}}\sum_{abcd}\Bigl(\delta_{ab}\delta_{cd}+2\alpha\,\epsilon\,\delta_{ab}\delta_{cd}f_{abcd}-\alpha\,\epsilon^{2}\,\delta_{ab}\delta_{cd}\bigl(g_{abcd}+(1-2\alpha)f_{abcd}^{2}\bigr)\Bigr)\right]+O(\epsilon^{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\!\left[\frac{1}{(d_{L}d_{\chi})^{2}}\sum_{abcd}\Bigl(\delta_{ab}\delta_{cd}-\alpha\,\epsilon^{2}\,\delta_{ab}\delta_{cd}\,g_{abcd}\Bigr)\right]+O(\epsilon^{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\log\!\left[1-\frac{\alpha\,\epsilon^{2}}{(d_{L}d_{\chi})^{2}}\sum_{a,c}g_{aacc}\right]+O(\epsilon^{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{\alpha-1}\,\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{(d_{L}d_{\chi})^{2}}\sum_{a,c}g_{aacc}+O(\epsilon^{3}).

(299)

where we have used the fact that
\delta_{ab}\delta_{cd}f_{abcd}=0
, since
P_{a}^{2}=I
.

Using the identity
\frac{1}{d}\sum_{a}P_{a}OP_{a}=\Tr(O)I
, we can further simplify the sum to:

\displaystyle\sum_{ac}g_{aacc}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{d_{L}d_{\chi}}\sum_{ac}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height]{theory_draft_images/SRE_diagrams/PsW_RW_R.png}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height]{theory_draft_images/SRE_diagrams/W_RW_RPs.png}}}-2\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height]{theory_draft_images/SRE_diagrams/W_RPsW_R.png}}}

(300)

\displaystyle=2(dd_{\chi})\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height]{theory_draft_images/SRE_diagrams/W_RW_R.png }}}-\frac{1}{d_{L}d_{\chi}}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height]{theory_draft_images/SRE_diagrams/W_Rsquared.png}}}\right).

Substitute it into Eq. (
299
) we obtain

\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}(\ket{V^{(\epsilon)}})=\frac{2\alpha}{\alpha-1}\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{d_{L}}\left(\Tr(\langle W_{R}^{2}\rangle_{\chi})-\frac{1}{d_{L}d_{\chi}}\Tr(\langle W_{R}\rangle_{\chi})^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3}),

(301)

where
\langle W_{R}\rangle_{\chi}:=\bra{\chi}W_{R}\ket{\chi}
, with
\ket{\chi}_{A_{2}\bar{A}}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{\chi}}}\sum_{i}\ket{i}_{A_{2}}\ket{i}_{\bar{A}_{2}}
.

Finally, we rewrite the SRE using
J
and
D
tensors defined in Eq. (
153
):

\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}(\ket{V^{(\epsilon)}})=\frac{\alpha}{2(\alpha-1)}\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{d_{L}d_{\chi}}\left(\Tr(J^{2})-\frac{1}{d_{L}d_{\chi}}\Tr(J)^{2}+\Tr(D^{2})\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3}).

(302)

Next, we use this formula to compute the amount of perturbative tripartite non-local magic in the state
\ket{V^{(\epsilon)}}
.

B.1
Case I:
|\bar{A}_{1}|=0

In this case, we have
\dim\mathcal{H}_{L}=\dim\mathcal{L}_{a}=d
, and
\bar{d}=\dim\mathcal{L}_{\bar{a}}=1
. The perturbative tripartite non-local magic is defined as the minimal magic that cannot be removed by the 2-local unitaries.
In the case when
\bar{A}_{2}
is empty, the 2-local unitary is parametrized as

\displaystyle\exp\left\{i\epsilon\left(T_{A_{1}A_{2}}\otimes I_{\bar{A}}+T_{A_{1}\bar{A}}\otimes I_{A_{2}}+T_{A_{2}\bar{A}}\otimes I_{A_{1}}\right)\right\}

(303)

The
J
term can be set to 0 by shifting
W_{R}\rightarrow W_{R}+T_{A_{1}A_{2}}\otimes I_{\bar{A}}+T_{A_{1}\bar{A}}\otimes I_{A_{2}}
, corresponding to the local unitary transformation on
A_{1}A_{2}
and
A_{1}\bar{A}
, as discussed in the last section;
D
is unchanged under this shift. The only 2-local transformation left is the shift
I_{A_{1}}\otimes T_{A_{2}\bar{A}}
, which changes
D
by
D\rightarrow D+I_{A_{1}}\otimes T^{D}_{A_{2}\bar{A}}
, with
T^{D}
defined as

\displaystyle T^{D}:=id_{\chi}\Tr_{A_{2}}\left([T,\chi]\right).

(304)

After setting
J=0
and include this shift to SRE, we find

\mathcal{M}^{(3)}_{\alpha}(\ket{V^{(\epsilon)}})=\frac{\alpha}{2(\alpha-1)}\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{dd_{\chi}}\min_{T^{D}}\Tr\left((D+I\otimes T^{D})^{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3}).

(305)

To find the
T^{D}
that minimizes the nonlocal magic, we expand
D
on
\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A_{2}\bar{A}}

D=I\otimes\delta_{2}+\delta_{1}\otimes I+\sum_{a}\delta_{1}^{a}\otimes\delta_{2}^{a}

(306)

where each
\delta^{a}_{i}
are traceless. The expression of the trace becomes

\Tr\left((D+I\otimes T^{D})^{2}\right)=d\Tr\left((\delta_{2}+T^{D})^{2}\right)+d_{\chi}\Tr(\delta_{1}^{2})+\sum_{a}\Tr((\delta_{1}^{a})^{2})\Tr((\delta_{2}^{a})^{2}).

(307)

Clearly, picking

T^{D}=-\delta_{2}=-\frac{1}{d}\operatorname{Tr}_{A_{2}\bar{A}}(D)

minimizes Eq. (
305
). Substituting it back, we find

\mathcal{M}^{(3)}\bigl(\ket{V^{(\epsilon)}}\bigr)=\frac{\alpha}{2(\alpha-1)}\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{dd_{\chi}}\left[\operatorname{Tr_{A_{1}A_{2}}}\!\big(D^{2}\big)-\frac{1}{d}\,\operatorname{Tr}_{A_{2}}\!\left(\operatorname{Tr}_{A_{1}}\!\big(D\big)^{2}\right)\right]+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3}).

(308)

This is exactly
c_{2}
(see Eq. (
99
)) up to constant prefactors. This proves Theorem
5.1
.

B.2
Case II:
|\bar{A}_{1}|>0

The case with nontrivial
\bar{A}_{1}
is analogous by treating
A_{1}
and
\bar{A}_{1}
equivalent with their respective reference systems. Now we have
d_{L}=d\bar{d}
. The 2-local perturbation can be on any pair in
\{A_{1},A_{2},\bar{A}_{1},\bar{A}_{2}\}
. To the
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\bigl(\ket{V^{(\epsilon)}}\bigr)=\frac{\alpha}{2(\alpha-1)}\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{d\bar{d}d_{\chi}}\left(\operatorname{Tr}(J^{2})-\frac{1}{d\bar{d}d_{\chi}}\operatorname{Tr}(J^{2})+\operatorname{Tr}(D^{2})\right),

(309)

where
d
,
\bar{d}
and
d_{\chi}
are the dimensions of
\mathcal{L}_{a}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\bar{a}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{A_{2}}
(same size as
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{A}_{2}}
). To see how
\mathcal{M}
is minimized in the equivalent class under 2-site local unitaries, we decompose
J
and
D
in terms of the Pauli operators on
A_{1}
,
\bar{A}_{1}
and
A_{2}\cup\bar{A}_{2}
:

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{d^{2}-1}\sum_{s=0}^{\bar{d}^{2}-1}\sum_{t=0}^{d_{\chi}^{2}-1}p_{rst}(J)P_{r}P_{s}P_{t}

(310)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{d^{2}-1}\sum_{s=0}^{\bar{d}^{2}-1}\sum_{t=0}^{d_{\chi}^{2}-1}p_{rst}(D)P_{r}P_{s}P_{t}

(311)

Plugging these expressions back in gives:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}\bigl(\ket{V^{(\epsilon)}}\bigr)=\frac{\alpha}{2(\alpha-1)}\epsilon^{2}\sum_{r=0}^{d^{2}-1}\sum_{s=0}^{\bar{d}^{2}-1}\sum_{t=0}^{d_{\chi}^{2}-1}\left(p_{rst}^{2}(J)+p_{000}^{2}(J)+p_{rst}^{2}(D)\right).

(312)

This is a sum of quadratic functions. Now we show that some of the terms in this sum can be removed by applying 2-local unitaries, thereby minimizing SRE of the code. For example, a carefully chosen 2-site unitary on
A_{1}\bar{A}_{1}
, in the form of
e^{i\epsilon T_{A_{1}\bar{A}_{1}}}
, can dial value of
c_{rs0}(J)
with
r,s>0
to zero. Because this transformation shift
W_{R}
by

\displaystyle W_{R}\rightarrow W_{R}+T_{A_{1}\bar{A}_{1}}\otimes I_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}}.

(313)

This transform
J
and
D
by

\displaystyle J\rightarrow

\displaystyle J+2T_{A_{1}\bar{A}_{1}}\otimes I_{A_{2}}

(314)

\displaystyle D\rightarrow

\displaystyle D.

This transformation only changes the Pauli coefficients
c_{rs0}(J)
and can change it to any value. Therefore to minimize SRE we set this coefficient to zero.

More generally we have the following list of two local unitary generators and the corresponding Pauli coefficients that they could affect:

\displaystyle T_{A_{1}A_{2}}:p_{r0t}(J),\qquad T_{A_{1}\bar{A}_{1}}:p_{rs0}(J),\qquad T_{A_{1}\bar{A}_{2}}:p_{r0t}(J),\qquad T_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{1}}:p_{0st}(J),

(315)

\displaystyle T_{\bar{A}_{1}\bar{A}_{2}}:p_{0st}(J),\qquad T_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}}:p_{00t}(J),\ p_{00t}(D).

By this token, after setting the corresponding coefficients to zero we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(3)}_{\alpha}\bigl(\ket{V^{(\epsilon)}}\bigr)=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha\epsilon^{2}}{2(\alpha-1)}\sum_{r>0,s>0,t>0}\left(p_{r0t}^{2}(D)+p_{0st}^{2}(D)+p_{rst}^{2}(D)+p_{rst}^{2}(J)\right)

(316)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha\epsilon^{2}}{2(\alpha-1)}\left(C_{D}+B_{D}+E_{D}+E_{J}\right),

(317)

according to the definition in Eq. (
160
) and Eq. (
166
). Since these coefficients are still positive after all possible 2-site unitary perturbations, we conclude that there is non-trivial non-local magic in a generically perturbed
\ket{V^{(\epsilon)}}
.
In terms of the coefficients
k_{i}
’s, this is

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(3)}_{\alpha}\bigl(\ket{V^{(\epsilon)}}\bigr)=

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha\epsilon^{2}}{(\alpha-1)}\left((1-\frac{1}{d^{2}})(1-\frac{1}{\bar{d}^{2}})(k_{3}+k_{4})+(1-\frac{1}{d^{2}})k_{2}+(1-\frac{1}{\bar{d}^{2}})k_{5}\right)

(318)

Here we provide some intuition for why local or bipartite forms of magic do not produce a state-dependent proto-area. First, any local non-Clifford deformation of the code of the form

\displaystyle V\rightarrow O_{A}O_{A}^{\dagger}VO_{A}^{\dagger}O_{\bar{A}}^{\dagger},

(319)

can be absorbed into the recovery unitaries
R_{A}
and
R_{\bar{A}}
. Such deformations therefore leave both the boundary entropy and the bulk entropy unchanged.

Moreover, deformations that act separately on
A_{1}\bar{A}_{1}
and on
A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}
transform the bulk matter degrees of freedom
\ket{\psi}_{L}
and the geometric auxiliary state
\ket{\chi}
independently, without generating correlations between them. As discussed in Sec.
2
, such matter–geometry correlations are necessary ingredients for nontrivial state dependence of the (proto-)area. We do not have a simple argument for why other bipartite unitaries do not produce state-dependence except by referring to the technical proof above.

Appendix C
Monotonicity of functions

C.1
Monotonicity of
f_{1}

Flat
\chi
spectrum

We will show that
f_{1}
is a monotonic function of
\theta_{k}\in(0,\pi/4)
, where
\theta_{k}
is an arbitrary angle defined in (
93
). We have defined
f_{1}
in (
91
), which can be rewritten in terms of the spectrum of
\sigma^{(L)}
(
95
) as

\displaystyle f_{1}(\{\lambda\})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2^{n}}\Tr({\ln\sigma^{(L)}})+S(\sigma^{(L)})

(320)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}-\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\log p_{k}+\log(1-p_{k})\bigr)-\bigl(p_{k}\log p_{k}+(1-p_{k})\log(1-p_{k})\bigr)

(321)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(p_{k}-\frac{1}{2})\log\frac{p_{k}}{1-p_{k}}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}g(p_{k}),

(322)

where
S
is the classical Shannon entropy
\sigma^{(L)}
and we used the fact that the product structure (
94
) implies that the two terms are the sum of the function of the single qubit spectrum. Now we only need to show the monotonicity of
g(p(\theta)).

A direct differentiation gives

g^{\prime}(p)=\ln\frac{p}{1-p}+\frac{2p-1}{2p(1-p)}>0,

(323)

when
p\in(1/2,1)
and
g^{\prime}(1/2)=0
. On the other hand, we have
p(\theta)=\cos^{2}\theta\in[1/2,1)
when
\theta\in(0,\pi/4]
, which gives

\frac{dp}{d\theta}=-\sin(2\theta)<0.

(324)

By the chain rule, the following inequality holds:

\frac{d}{d\theta}\,g(p(\theta))=g^{\prime}(p(\theta))\,\frac{dp}{d\theta}<0\qquad\text{for }\theta\in(0,\pi/4],

(325)

with strict inequality for
\theta\in(0,\pi/4)
. ∎

Non-flat
\chi
spectrum

In the general setting of Theorems (
4.5
) and (
4.6
), we defined the quantity
f_{1}^{mn}
in Eq. (
288
) by

f_{1}^{mn}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2d}\sum_{i,j}\frac{\left(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}\right)^{2}}{\left(\mu_{m}\lambda_{i}-\mu_{n}\lambda_{j}\right)}\ln\frac{\mu_{m}\lambda_{i}}{\mu_{n}\lambda_{j}}\,.

(326)

The expression is continuous, with the apparent singularity at
\mu_{m}\lambda_{i}=\mu_{n}\lambda_{j}
being removable by taking the corresponding limit. We will show that
f_{1}^{mn}
is a monotonic function of
\theta_{k}\in(0,\pi/4)
, where
\theta_{k}
is an arbitrary angle introduced in (
93
). We also use the same parametrization of the eigenvalues introduced in Eq. (
95
)
Since
\mu_{m}\lambda_{i}>0
and
\mu_{n}\lambda_{j}>0
, use

\ln\frac{x}{y}=\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\left(\frac{1}{s+y}-\frac{1}{s+x}\right),

(327)

Then

f_{1}^{mn}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2d}\sum_{i,j}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,g^{mn}_{s}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}),\qquad g^{mn}_{s}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}):=\frac{(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})^{2}}{(s+\mu_{m}\lambda_{i})(s+\mu_{n}\lambda_{j})}.

(328)

where
It suffices to prove that, for each fixed
s
, the integrand is pointwise monotonic in
\theta_{k}\in(0,\pi/4]
. To this end, rewrite the spectrum
\{\lambda_{i}\}
as an explicit function of
p:=p_{k}
, keeping all remaining factors
(\ell\neq k)
in (
95
) fixed. The spectrum then decomposes into two families,

\{\,p\,\omega_{\beta},\ (1-p)\,\omega_{\beta}\,\}_{\beta\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}},

where the weight associated with the
(n-1)
-bit string
\beta
is

\omega_{\beta}=\prod_{\ell\neq k}p_{\ell}^{\,1-\beta_{\ell}}\bigl(1-p_{\ell}\bigr)^{\beta_{\ell}}.

(329)

where
\beta_{i}
denotes the
i
-th bit (component) of the string
\beta
.

Since each argument of
g^{mn}_{s}
may come from either eigenvalue family, we can split the double sum into the four possible group pairings:

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}g^{mn}_{s}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta,\tau\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}}\Big[g^{mn}_{s}(p\omega_{\beta},\,p\omega_{\tau})+g^{mn}_{s}\big((1-p)\omega_{\beta},\,p\omega_{\tau}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+g^{mn}_{s}\big(p\omega_{\beta},\,(1-p)\omega_{\tau}\big)+g^{mn}_{s}\big((1-p)\omega_{\beta},\,(1-p)\omega_{\tau}\big)\Big],

(330)

Differentiating the integrand with respect to
p
yields

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p}\sum_{i,j}g_{s}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j})=(2p-1)\,\mathcal{}\Upsilon_{s}(p),

where we have introduced the shorthand

\Upsilon_{s}(p)=\sum_{\beta,\tau}\frac{X^{mn}_{\beta,\tau}(p;s)}{\Big[\bigl(\mu_{m}\,\omega_{\sigma}\,p+s\bigr)\bigl(\mu_{n}\,\omega_{\tau}\,p+s\bigr)\bigl(\mu_{m}\,\omega_{\sigma}(1-p)+s\bigr)\bigl(\mu_{n}\,\omega_{\tau}(1-p)+s\bigr)\Big]^{2}}.

(331)

with

\displaystyle X^{mn}_{\beta,\tau}(p;s)={}

\displaystyle\ \mu_{m}^{3}\,\omega_{\sigma}^{3}\,\omega_{\tau}^{2}\Big(\mu_{n}^{3}(p-1)^{2}p^{2}\,\omega_{\tau}\big(\omega_{\sigma}^{2}+\omega_{\tau}^{2}\big)-2\mu_{n}^{2}(p-1)p\,s\Big(-2(p-1)p\,\omega_{\sigma}\omega_{\tau}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt-(p-1)p\,\omega_{\sigma}^{2}+\big((p-1)p+1\big)\omega_{\tau}^{2}\Big)+\mu_{n}s^{2}\big(8(p-1)^{2}p^{2}\,\omega_{\sigma}+\omega_{\tau}\big)+s^{3}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt+\mu_{m}^{2}s\,\omega_{\sigma}^{2}\omega_{\tau}\Big(-2\mu_{n}^{3}(p-1)p\,\omega_{\tau}^{2}\Big(-2(p-1)p\,\omega_{\sigma}\omega_{\tau}+\big((p-1)p+1\big)\omega_{\sigma}^{2}-(p-1)p\,\omega_{\tau}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt-4\mu_{n}^{2}(p-1)p\,s\,\omega_{\tau}\Big(\big((p-1)p+1\big)\omega_{\sigma}^{2}+\big((p-1)p+1\big)\omega_{\tau}^{2}+3\omega_{\sigma}\omega_{\tau}\Big)+\mu_{n}s^{2}\omega_{\tau}\Big(2\big(1-

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt8(p-1)p\big)\omega_{\sigma}+3\omega_{\tau}\Big)+2s^{3}\big(\omega_{\sigma}+2\omega_{\tau}\big)\Big)+\mu_{m}s^{2}\omega_{\sigma}\Big(\mu_{n}^{3}\omega_{\sigma}\omega_{\tau}^{3}\big(8(p-1)^{2}p^{2}\omega_{\tau}+\omega_{\sigma}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip-34.1433pt+\mu_{n}^{2}s\,\omega_{\sigma}\omega_{\tau}^{2}\Big(2\big(1-8(p-1)p\big)\omega_{\tau}+3\omega_{\sigma}\Big)+\mu_{n}s^{2}\omega_{\tau}\Big(8\big(1-2(p-1)p\big)\omega_{\sigma}\omega_{\tau}+3\omega_{\sigma}^{2}+3\omega_{\tau}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt+2s^{3}\Big(2\omega_{\sigma}\omega_{\tau}+\omega_{\sigma}^{2}+3\omega_{\tau}^{2}\Big)\Big)+s^{3}\big(\mu_{n}\omega_{\tau}+2s\big)\Big(\mu_{n}^{2}\omega_{\sigma}^{2}\omega_{\tau}^{2}+2\mu_{n}s\,\omega_{\sigma}\omega_{\tau}(\omega_{\sigma}+\omega_{\tau})

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt+2s^{2}\big(\omega_{\sigma}^{2}+\omega_{\tau}^{2}\big)\Big).

(332)

A direct symbolic check in
Mathematica
shows that, for parameters in the range
0<\omega_{\beta},\omega_{\tau}<1
,
s\geq 0
,
\tfrac{1}{2}<p<1
,
0<\mu_{m},\mu_{n}<1
, the numerator
X^{mn}_{\beta,\tau}(p;s)
is strictly positive. Consequently the summand in
\Upsilon_{s}(p)
is positive term-by-term, and therefore

\Upsilon_{s}(p)>0\qquad\text{for all }p\in\Big(\tfrac{1}{2},1\Big).

Therefore, using Eq. (
324
) and the chain rule, for
\theta_{k}\in(0,\pi/4]
we obtain

\frac{\partial f_{1}^{mn}}{\partial\theta_{k}}=\frac{\partial f_{1}^{mn}}{\partial p}\,\frac{\partial p}{\partial\theta_{k}}<0.

(333)

∎

C.2
Monotonicity of
f_{2}

Flat
\chi
spectrum

Recall that we have defined (
91
):

f_{2}(\{\lambda_{i}\})=\left(\frac{1}{2^{n-1}}+\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}\frac{(\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j})^{2}}{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}\ln{\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{j}}}\right)-2

(334)

and we want to know that it is a monotonic function
\theta_{k}\in(0,\pi/4]
for all
k
.
To study the
\theta_{k}
–dependence of
f_{2}
, we use the same parametrization of the eigenvalues introduced in Eq. (
95
) and differentiate with respect to fixed
p_{k}
. Since
\lambda_{i}>0
, we substitute Eq (
343
) in Eq (
334
) and the part of
f_{2}
in the parenthesis can be written as:

\displaystyle F(\{\lambda\})=\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\sum_{i,j}\frac{(\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j})^{2}}{(s+\lambda_{i})(s+\lambda_{j})}:=\sum_{i,j}\int_{0}^{\infty}dsh_{s}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}).

(335)

where we have defined

h_{s}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j})=\frac{(\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j})^{2}}{(s+\lambda_{i})(s+\lambda_{j})}

(336)

It suffices to show that the integrand is a point-wise monotonic function of
\theta_{k}\in(0,\pi/4]
in
s
. Rewrite the spectrum
\lambda
as an explicit function of
p:=p_{k}
for a fixed
k
and all the other factors
(i\neq k)
in (
95
). Then we can split the spectrum into two groups:
\{p\omega_{\beta}
,
(1-p)\omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}}
where the
(n-1)
-bit string dependent
\omega_{\beta}
is defined as Eq (
329
). Since the two arguments of
h_{s}
can come from either group of eigenvalues, the sum can be split up into 4 terms that contains all pairs of group inputs, as follows

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}h_{s}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta,\tau\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}}\Bigg[h_{s}(p\omega_{\beta},p\omega_{\tau})+h_{s}((1-p)\omega_{\beta},p\omega_{\tau}))+h_{s}(p\omega_{\beta},(1-p)\omega_{\tau})

(337)

\displaystyle+h_{s}((1-p)\omega_{\beta},(1-p)\omega_{\tau})\Bigg]

(338)

Differentiate the integrand with respect to
p
gives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial p}\sum_{i,j}h_{s}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j})=(2p-1)\,\Phi_{s}(p),

where

\Phi_{s}(p,s)=\sum_{\beta,\tau}\frac{K_{\beta,\tau}(p;s)}{\Big(\left(-pw_{\beta}+s+w_{\beta}\right)\left(pw_{\beta}+s\right)\left(-pw_{\tau}+s+w_{\tau}\right)\left(pw_{\tau}+s\right)\Big)^{2}}

(339)

and

\displaystyle K_{\beta,\tau}(p;s)=

\displaystyle(p-1)^{2}p^{2}w_{\beta}^{5}w_{\tau}^{2}\left(2s+w_{\tau}\right)+2sw_{\beta}^{2}\left(\left(8p^{2}-8p+1\right)s^{2}w_{\tau}^{3}+(p-1)^{2}p^{2}w_{\tau}^{5}\right.

(340)

\displaystyle\left.+2p\left(-3p^{3}+6p^{2}-4p+1\right)sw_{\tau}^{4}+8(p-1)ps^{3}w_{\tau}^{2}+2s^{5}+s^{4}w_{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle+w_{\beta}^{3}\left(2\left(8p^{2}-8p+1\right)s^{3}w_{\tau}^{2}+2\left(6p^{2}-6p+1\right)s^{2}w_{\tau}^{3}+(p-1)^{2}p^{2}w_{\tau}^{5}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+2p\left(-3p^{3}+6p^{2}-4p+1\right)sw_{\tau}^{4}+2s^{5}+s^{4}w_{\tau}\right)+s^{4}w_{\beta}w_{\tau}^{2}\left(2s+w_{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle-2p\left(3p^{3}-6p^{2}+4p-1\right)sw_{\beta}^{4}w_{\tau}^{2}\left(2s+w_{\tau}\right)+2s^{5}w_{\tau}^{2}\left(2s+w_{\tau}\right).

One can verify that
K_{\beta,\tau}(p;s)
is a strictly positive function for all

0<w_{\beta},w_{\tau}<1
,
s\geq 0
, and
1/2<p<1
, using
Mathematica
and
\Phi_{s}(p)>0
for
p\in(1/2,1)
.
Hence, by chain rule, when
p\in(1/2,1)
or
\theta\in(0,\pi/4]
,

\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial\theta}=\frac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial p}\frac{\partial p}{\partial\theta}<0

(341)

where we have applied equation (
324
). ∎

Non flat
\chi
spectrum

We define

f_{2}^{mn}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2d}\sum_{ij}\frac{(\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j})^{2}}{\mu_{m}\lambda_{i}-\mu_{n}\lambda_{j}}\ln\frac{\mu_{m}\lambda_{i}}{\mu_{n}\lambda_{j}}

(342)

We understand the potentially singular terms with
\mu_{m}\lambda_{i}=\mu_{n}\lambda_{j}
by continuous extension, which is finite and well-defined. Substituting the following

\ln\frac{x}{y}=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{x-y}{s\,x+(1-s)\,y}\,ds,\qquad x,y>0.

(343)

into (
291
), we get

\displaystyle f_{2}^{mn}(p)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2d}\int_{0}^{1}ds\sum_{i,j}\Bigg(\frac{(\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j})^{2}}{s\,\mu_{m}\lambda_{i}+(1-s)\,\mu_{n}\lambda_{j}}\Bigg)=\frac{1}{2d}\int_{0}^{1}ds\;\sum_{i,j}h^{mn}_{s}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}),

(344)

where we define

h^{mn}_{s}(x,y):=\frac{(x+y)^{2}}{s\,\mu_{m}x+(1-s)\,\mu_{n}y}.

(345)

Next, as in the flat-spectrum case, we partition the index set for
{\lambda_{i}}
into the two subsets corresponding to
\{p\,\omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}}
and
\{(1-p)\,\omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle f_{2}^{mn}(p)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2d}\int_{0}^{1}ds\;\sum_{\beta,\gamma}\Big[h^{mn}_{s}\!\big(p\omega_{\beta},\,p\omega_{\gamma}\big)+h^{mn}_{s}\!\big(p\omega_{\beta},\,(1-p)\omega_{\gamma}\big)+h^{mn}_{s}\!\big((1-p)\omega_{\beta},\,p\omega_{\gamma}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+h^{mn}_{s}\!\big((1-p)\omega_{\beta},\,(1-p)\omega_{\gamma}\big)\Big].

(346)

For each fixed
s\in(0,1)
and
(\beta,\gamma)
, define the four-term block

\displaystyle F^{mn}_{s}(p;\beta,\gamma)

\displaystyle:=h^{mn}_{s}\!\big(p\omega_{\beta},\,p\omega_{\gamma}\big)+h^{mn}_{s}\!\big(p\omega_{\beta},\,(1-p)\omega_{\gamma}\big)+h^{mn}_{s}\!\big((1-p)\omega_{\beta},\,p\omega_{\gamma}\big)

(347)

\displaystyle\qquad+h^{mn}_{s}\!\big((1-p)\omega_{\beta},\,(1-p)\omega_{\gamma}\big).

(348)

By inspection,
F^{mn}_{s}(p;\beta,\gamma)=F^{mn}_{s}(1-p;\beta,\gamma)
, so
F^{mn}_{s}
is symmetric about
p=\tfrac{1}{2}
.
We now show that
F^{mn}_{s}(p;\beta,\gamma)
is convex in
p
. First note that the first and last terms in
(
348
) are affine in
p
:
Hence

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p^{2}}h^{mn}_{s}\!\big(p\omega_{\beta},\,p\omega_{\gamma}\big)=0,\qquad\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p^{2}}h^{mn}_{s}\!\big((1-p)\omega_{\beta},\,(1-p)\omega_{\gamma}\big)=0.

(349)

It remains to treat the two cross terms. Let

g(p):=h^{mn}_{s}\!\big(p\omega_{\beta},\,(1-p)\omega_{\gamma}\big)=\frac{\big((\omega_{\beta}-\omega_{\gamma})p+\omega_{\gamma}\big)^{2}}{\big(s\mu_{m}\omega_{\beta}-(1-s)\mu_{n}\omega_{\gamma}\big)p+(1-s)\mu_{n}\omega_{\gamma}}.

(350)

This has the form
g(p)=\dfrac{(ap+b)^{2}}{cp+d}
with
cp+d>0
, and therefore

g^{\prime\prime}(p)=\frac{2(ad-bc)^{2}}{(cp+d)^{3}}\geq 0,\qquad p\in[1/2,1).

(351)

The other cross term
h^{mn}_{s}\!\big((1-p)\omega_{\beta},\,p\omega_{\gamma}\big)
is obtained by swapping

\beta\leftrightarrow\gamma
and
p\leftrightarrow(1-p)
, hence it is convex as well.
Consequently,

F^{mn}_{s}(p;\beta,\gamma)
is convex in
p
for each fixed
(s,\beta,\gamma)
. Hence for each fixed
(s,\beta,\gamma)
, and sums/integrals preserve convexity, it follows from

f_{2}^{mn}(p)=\frac{1}{2d}\int_{0}^{1}ds\;\sum_{\beta,\gamma}F^{mn}_{s}(p;\beta,\gamma)

that
f_{2}^{mn}(p)
is convex in
p
. Moreover,
f_{2}^{mn}(p)
is symmetric under
p\leftrightarrow(1-p)
. Hence

f_{2}^{mn}(p)=f_{2}^{mn}(1-p).

(352)

For a convex function symmetric about
p=\frac{1}{2}
, the minimum is attained at
p=\frac{1}{2}
, and the function is nondecreasing on
[\frac{1}{2},1]
. Therefore
7
7

7

In particular, taking
\mu_{m}=\mu_{n}=1
recovers the function
f_{2}
considered in the flat case, so the above provides an independent second proof of convexity/monotonicity for
f_{2}
,

\frac{d}{dp}f_{2}^{mn}(p)\geq 0,\qquad p\in\Big[\frac{1}{2},1\Big).

(353)

∎

C.3
Monotonicity of
f_{3}

Flat
\chi
spectrum

We will employ the same strategy by singling out
p:=p_{k}
, dividing the spectrum into two groups

\{p\,\omega_{\beta},(1-p)\omega_{\beta}\}_{\beta\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}}
, and differentiating
f_{3}
with respect to
p
.

Notice that

f_{3}(\{\lambda_{i}(p)\}):=\sum_{i,j}\frac{\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j}}{2(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})}\ln\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{j}}

(354)

depends only on the ratios between the eigenvalues
\{\lambda_{i}\}
. Let

t_{ij}:=\lambda_{i}/\lambda_{j}

and define the function on the positive axis

g(t):=\frac{t+1}{2(t-1)}\ln t,\qquad g(1)=1

(355)

by continuity. Note that
g(t)=g(t^{-1})
, and

g^{\prime}(t)=\frac{t^{2}-1-2t\log t}{2(t-1)^{2}t}.

(356)

Now the monotonicity of
f_{3}
can be repackaged in terms of all possible ratios between pairs of eigenvalues:

\displaystyle f_{3}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma,\tau\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}}g\!\left(\frac{\omega_{\sigma}}{\omega_{\tau}}\right)+g\!\left(\frac{\omega_{\sigma}p}{\omega_{\tau}(1-p)}\right)+g\!\left(\frac{\omega_{\sigma}(1-p)}{\omega_{\tau}p}\right)+g\!\left(\frac{\omega_{\tau}}{\omega_{\sigma}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma,\tau\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}}2\,g\!\left(\frac{\omega_{\sigma}}{\omega_{\tau}}\right)+g\!\left(\frac{\omega_{\sigma}p}{\omega_{\tau}(1-p)}\right)+g\!\left(\frac{\omega_{\sigma}(1-p)}{\omega_{\tau}p}\right).

(357)

Let

t:=\frac{p}{1-p}.

(358)

Then
t\geq 1
for
p\in[1/2,1)
. We will show that the derivative of
f_{3}
with respect to
p
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f_{3}}{\partial p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma,\tau\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}}\frac{1}{p(1-p)}\left[\frac{\omega_{\sigma}}{\omega_{\tau}}\,t\,g^{\prime}\!\left(\frac{\omega_{\sigma}}{\omega_{\tau}}\,t\right)-\frac{\omega_{\sigma}}{\omega_{\tau}}\,\frac{1}{t}\,g^{\prime}\!\left(\frac{\omega_{\sigma}}{\omega_{\tau}}\,\frac{1}{t}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p(1-p)}\sum_{\sigma,\tau\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}}\left[h\!\left(\frac{\omega_{\sigma}}{\omega_{\tau}}\,t\right)-h\!\left(\frac{\omega_{\sigma}}{\omega_{\tau}}\,\frac{1}{t}\right)\right],

(359)

takes a definite sign on
p\in(1/2,1]
by showing that
h(u):=u\,g^{\prime}(u)

is monotone on
u\in(0,\infty)
. Since
\frac{\omega_{\sigma}}{\omega_{\tau}}>0
and
t\geq 1
, we have

\frac{\omega_{\sigma}}{\omega_{\tau}}\,t\;\geq\;\frac{\omega_{\sigma}}{\omega_{\tau}}\,\frac{1}{t},

so if
h
is increasing, then each bracket term in (
359
) is nonnegative. By definition,

h(t)=\frac{t^{2}-1-2t\log t}{2(t-1)^{2}},

(360)

and its derivative is

h^{\prime}(t)=\frac{t+1}{(t-1)^{3}}\left(\log t-\frac{2(t-1)}{t+1}\right)=:\frac{t+1}{(t-1)^{3}}\,q(t).

(361)

Now

q^{\prime}(t)=\frac{(t-1)^{2}}{t(t+1)^{2}}\geq 0,\qquad q(1)=0.

(362)

Hence
q(t)\leq 0
for
0<t<1
and
q(t)\geq 0
for
t>1
. Since
q(t)
has the same sign as
(t-1)
, it follows that

h^{\prime}(t)\geq 0\qquad\text{for all }t>0.

(363)

Therefore
h
is increasing on
(0,\infty)
, and thus every bracket term in (
359
) is nonnegative when

p\in(1/2,1]
. This shows that

\frac{\partial f_{3}}{\partial p}\geq 0\qquad\text{for }p\in(1/2,1].

(364)

By the chain rule,

\frac{df_{3}}{d\theta_{k}}=\frac{df_{3}}{dp_{k}}\,\frac{dp_{k}}{d\theta_{k}}\leq 0

(365)

when
\theta_{k}\in(0,\pi/4]
as before. ∎

Non-flat
\chi
spectrum

We define

f_{3}^{mn}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2d}\sum_{ij}\mathcal{P}^{mn}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}),\qquad\mathcal{P}^{mn}(x,y):=\frac{x+y}{2(\mu_{m}x-\mu_{n}y)}\ln\frac{\mu_{m}x}{\mu_{n}y},

(366)

with
\mathcal{P}^{mn}(x,x)
understood by continuous extension. Fix
(m,n)
and set
\gamma^{mn}:=\mu_{n}/\mu_{m}
. Since
\mathcal{P}^{mn}(cx,cy)=\mathcal{P}^{mn}(x,y)
for all
c>0
,
it depends only on the ratio
t:=\frac{x}{y}>0
.

Define the associated one-variable function

g^{mn}(t):=\frac{t+1}{2(t-\gamma^{mn})}\,\ln\!\Big(\frac{t}{\gamma^{mn}}\Big),\qquad t>0,\qquad g^{mn}(\gamma^{mn}):=\frac{\gamma^{mn}+1}{2\gamma^{mn}},

(367)

so that

\mathcal{P}^{mn}(x,y)=\frac{1}{\mu_{m}}\,g^{mn}\!\Big(\frac{x}{y}\Big),\qquad f_{3}^{mn}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2d\,\mu_{m}}\sum_{i,j}g^{mn}\!\Big(\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{j}}\Big).

(368)

Fix an index
k
and vary
p:=p_{k}\in[1/2,1)
, holding all other
p_{\ell}
fixed. As in the flat-spectrum case, the
spectrum can be written as

\{\,w_{\sigma}p,\;w_{\sigma}(1-p)\,\}_{\sigma\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}},

where
w_{\sigma}>0
depends only on
\{p_{\ell}\}_{\ell\neq k}
. For each ordered pair
(\sigma,\tau)
define the ratio

n_{\sigma\tau}:=\frac{w_{\sigma}}{w_{\tau}}>0.

Then the contribution of the four ratios formed by
\{w_{\sigma}p,w_{\sigma}(1-p)\}
against
\{w_{\tau}p,w_{\tau}(1-p)\}

can be packaged into the two-level quantity

\mathcal{Q}^{mn}_{n}(p):=\sum_{a,b\in\{0,1\}}g^{mn}\!\Big(n\,\frac{q_{a}}{q_{b}}\Big),\qquad(q_{0}=p,\ q_{1}=1-p),

(369)

and hence

f_{3}^{mn}(p)=\frac{1}{2d\,\mu_{m}}\sum_{\sigma,\tau}\mathcal{Q}^{mn}_{n_{\sigma\tau}}(p).

(370)

Expanding (
369
) gives

\mathcal{Q}^{mn}_{n}(p)=2\,g^{mn}(n)+g^{mn}\!\Big(n\,\frac{p}{1-p}\Big)+g^{mn}\!\Big(n\,\frac{1-p}{p}\Big).

(371)

The first term is independent of
p
, so it suffices to control the last two terms. Let

t:=\frac{p}{1-p}\geq 1\quad\text{for }p\in\Big[\frac{1}{2},1\Big),\qquad\text{so that}\qquad\frac{dt}{dp}=\frac{t}{p(1-p)}.

(372)

Differentiating (
371
) using (
372
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dp}\mathcal{Q}^{mn}_{n}(p)

\displaystyle=\frac{t}{p(1-p)}\left[n\,(g^{mn})^{\prime}(nt)-n\,t^{-2}(g^{mn})^{\prime}(n/t)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p(1-p)}\left[h^{mn}(nt)-h^{mn}(n/t)\right],

(373)

where we define

h^{mn}(x):=x\,(g^{mn})^{\prime}(x).

(374)

Thus
\frac{d}{dp}\mathcal{Q}^{mn}_{n}(p)
has a definite sign on
p\in[1/2,1)
once we know that
h^{mn}
is monotone
increasing on
(0,\infty)
, since
t\geq 1
implies
nt\geq n/t
.

A direct computation gives

(h^{mn})^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1+\gamma^{mn}}{2(\gamma^{mn})^{2}}\,\frac{(u+1)\ln u-2(u-1)}{(u-1)^{3}}.

(375)

where we defined
u:=x/\gamma^{mn}>0
. Define
q(u):=\ln u-\frac{2(u-1)}{u+1}
. One checks

q^{\prime}(u)=\frac{(u-1)^{2}}{u(u+1)^{2}}\geq 0,\qquad q(1)=0,

so
q(u)\leq 0
for
0<u<1
and
q(u)\geq 0
for
u>1
. Since

(u+1)\ln u-2(u-1)=(u+1)q(u)

has the same sign as
(u-1)
, the fraction in (
375
) is nonnegative for all
u>0
. Hence

(h^{mn})^{\prime}(x)\geq 0\quad\text{for all }x>0,

(376)

so
h^{mn}
is increasing on
(0,\infty)
. Therefore, for
p\in[1/2,1)
we have
t\geq 1
and thus
nt\geq n/t
, implying from (
373
) that

\frac{d}{dp}\mathcal{Q}^{mn}_{n}(p)\geq 0.

Summing over
(\sigma,\tau)
preserves monotonicity, and (
370
) gives

\frac{d}{dp}f_{3}^{mn}(p)\geq 0,\qquad p\in\Big[\frac{1}{2},1\Big).

(377)

Finally, with
p_{k}=\cos^{2}\theta_{k}
and
\theta_{k}\in(0,\pi/4]
, we have
\frac{dp_{k}}{d\theta_{k}}=-\sin(2\theta_{k})\leq 0
, and thus

\frac{df_{3}^{mn}}{d\theta_{k}}=\frac{df_{3}^{mn}}{dp_{k}}\,\frac{dp_{k}}{d\theta_{k}}\leq 0,

as claimed. ∎

Trace diagram dictionary

and

\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}}
,
\mathcal{H}_{A_{2}}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{A}}
,
respectively. If a reference leg is present, it is denoted
r
and corresponds
to the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{r}
. In that case, the ordering becomes

\mathcal{H}_{r}
,

,

,

\mathcal{H}_{\bar{A}}
from outermost to innermost.
