Appendix E
Technical details

E.1
Perturbative expansion of the entropy

We are interested in the perturbative expansion of the entropy functional

S\!\left(A(\epsilon)\right):=-\Tr\!\left(A(\epsilon)\log A(\epsilon)\right)

(378)

about
\epsilon=0
. A key technical input is an explicit expression for the
\epsilon
-derivative of the operator logarithm in terms of resolvents. Before turning to the entropy expansion, we therefore recall (and for completeness derive) the standard resolvent representation for
\frac{d}{d\epsilon}\log A(\epsilon)
.

Lemma E.1
.

For any differentiable family of positive operators
A(\epsilon)
, the derivative of the logarithm
admits the resolvent representation

\frac{d}{d\epsilon}\,\log A(\epsilon)=\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\bigl[A(\epsilon)+sI\bigr]^{-1}\frac{dA(\epsilon)}{d\epsilon}\,\bigl[A(\epsilon)+sI\bigr]^{-1},

(379)

which follows from functional calculus and holds without any commutativity assumptions.

Proof.

The following proof follows standard arguments based on functional calculus and
resolvent identities (see, e.g.,
34
). We include it here for
completeness. We start from the standard integral identity for positive operators,

\ln(A+B)-\ln A=\int_{0}^{\infty}du\,\Bigl[(A+uI)^{-1}-(A+B+uI)^{-1}\Bigr].

(380)

Let
A(t)>0
be a differentiable family. By definition of the derivative,

\frac{d}{dt}\ln A(t)=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\ln A(t+h)-\ln A(t)}{h}.

(381)

Using the first-order expansion
A(t+h)=A(t)+h\dot{A}(t)+O(h^{2})
and setting

A:=A(t)
,
B:=h\dot{A}(t)
in (
380
), we obtain

\frac{d}{dt}\ln A(t)=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}\int_{0}^{\infty}du\,\Bigl[(A+uI)^{-1}-(A+h\dot{A}(t)+uI)^{-1}\Bigr].

(382)

For infinitesimal
h
, we expand the resolvent as follows:

\displaystyle(A+h\dot{A}+uI)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\Bigl[(A+uI)\bigl(I+h(A+uI)^{-1}\dot{A}\bigr)\Bigr]^{-1}

\displaystyle=\bigl(I+h(A+uI)^{-1}\dot{A}\bigr)^{-1}(A+uI)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(I-h(A+uI)^{-1}\dot{A}+O(h^{2})\Bigr)(A+uI)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(A+uI)^{-1}-h(A+uI)^{-1}\dot{A}\,(A+uI)^{-1}+O(h^{2}),

(383)

where
\dot{A}:=dA/dt
. Substituting (
383
) into
(
382
) and taking the limit
h\to 0
yields the
resolvent representation for the derivative of the logarithm:

\frac{d}{dt}\ln A(t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}du\,(A(t)+uI)^{-1}\,\dot{A}(t)\,(A(t)+uI)^{-1}.

(384)

∎

Since
A(\epsilon)>0
for
\epsilon
in a neighborhood of
0
, the entropy admits
the Taylor expansion

S\!\left(A(\epsilon)\right)=S\!\left(A(0)\right)+\epsilon\,\left.\frac{d}{d\epsilon}S\!\left(A(\epsilon)\right)\right|_{\epsilon=0}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}\,\left.\frac{d^{2}}{d\epsilon^{2}}S\!\left(A(\epsilon)\right)\right|_{\epsilon=0}+O(\epsilon^{3}).

(385)

We now specialize to the perturbative ansatz

A(\epsilon)=A^{(0)}+\epsilon A^{(1)}+\epsilon^{2}A^{(2)}+O(\epsilon^{3}),

(386)

with
A^{(0)}>0
. Using (
384
), the entropy admits the expansion

\displaystyle S\!\left(A(\epsilon)\right)

\displaystyle=-\Tr\!\left[A^{(0)}\log A^{(0)}\right]-\epsilon\,\Tr\!\left(A^{(1)}\log A^{(0)}+A^{(0)}\,D_{\ln}\!\bigl(A^{(0)}\bigr)\!\left[A^{(1)}\right]\right)

(387)

\displaystyle-\epsilon^{2}\,\Tr\Bigl(A^{(2)}\log A^{(0)}+A^{(1)}\,D_{\ln}\!\bigl(A^{(0)}\bigr)\!\left[A^{(1)}\right]+A^{(0)}\,D_{\ln}\!\bigl(A^{(0)}\bigr)\!\left[A^{(2)}\right]\Bigr)

\displaystyle+O(\epsilon^{3}).

Here
D_{\ln}(A)
and
D^{2}_{\ln}(A)
denote the first and second Fréchet
derivatives of the matrix logarithm, which admit the standard resolvent
representations

\displaystyle D_{\ln}(A)[X]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}(A+sI)^{-1}\,X\,(A+sI)^{-1}\,ds,

(388)

\displaystyle D^{2}_{\ln}(A)[X,Y]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}(A+sI)^{-1}\,X\,(A+sI)^{-1}\,Y\,(A+sI)^{-1}\,ds.

We have also used the identity in Eq. (
390
) to obtain Eq. (
387
). All traces in (
387
) are well defined provided the integrands are
trace class, which is automatically satisfied in finite-dimensional settings.

E.2
Identity relating
D_{\ln}
and
D_{\ln}^{2}

Lemma E.2
.

Let
A>0
be an invertible matrix and let
B
be any matrix of the same dimension. Then

\Tr\!\left(A\,D^{2}_{\ln}(A)[B,B]\right)=\frac{1}{2}\,\Tr\!\left(B\,D_{\ln}(A)[B]\right).

(389)

Proof.

Using the resolvent representations Eq. (
388
), Eq. (
389
) reduces to the resolvent trace identity

\,\Tr\!\left(A\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,ds\right)=\Tr\!\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,ds\right).

(390)

Now, we also have

\displaystyle\Tr\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s}{(A+sI)^{2}}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,ds

\displaystyle=-\Tr\int_{0}^{\infty}s\,\frac{d}{ds}\!\left(\frac{1}{A+sI}\right)\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,ds

(391)

\displaystyle=-\Tr\left[\left.s\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\right|_{s=0}^{s=\infty}\right]

\displaystyle-\Tr\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s}{(A+sI)^{2}}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,ds

\displaystyle+\Tr\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,ds.

The boundary term vanishes: as
s\to 0
the prefactor
s
kills the expression since
A>0

implies
(A+sI)^{-1}
bounded, and as
s\to\infty
we have
(A+sI)^{-1}=O(1/s)
so the traced
expression is
O(1/s)\to 0
. Therefore (
391
) implies

2\,\Tr\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s}{(A+sI)^{2}}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,ds=\Tr\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,ds.

(392)

Now,

\displaystyle\Tr\!\left(A\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,ds\right)=\,\Tr\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{A}{(A+sI)^{2}}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,ds

(393)

\displaystyle=\,\Tr\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,ds-\,\Tr\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s}{(A+sI)^{2}}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,ds

\displaystyle=\,\Tr\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,ds-\frac{1}{2}\Tr\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,ds

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Tr\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,\frac{1}{A+sI}\,B\,ds,

This proves (
390
), and hence (
389
).
∎

E.3
Diagrammatic Representation and Haar Integration

We adopt a diagrammatic convention for representing operators, states, and unitaries.
Each object in the computation is represented by a labeled box, with lines (or “legs”)
denoting the Hilbert spaces on which the object acts. The legs entering or exiting a box correspond
to input and output indices of the operator, respectively. Connecting two legs represents a
tensor contraction (i.e., a summation over the shared index), or equivalently, a partial trace
over the associated Hilbert space.

For example, consider the product of three operators
A
,
B
, and
C
acting on the same space.
In the diagrammatic notation, this corresponds to placing three boxes sequentially and connecting the output leg
of
A
to the input leg of
B
, and the output of
B
to the input of
C
.
The resulting chain of connections encodes the matrix multiplication
ABC
.

If we take the trace of this product,
\mathrm{Tr}(ABC)
, we close the remaining open legs by connecting
the output leg of
C
back to the input leg of
A
, forming a single closed loop.

This loop represents the summation over all contracted indices in the trace.
We adopt the following diagrammatic conventions (used throughout).

Diagram Conventions

•

Every closed loop in a diagram corresponds to a trace, and every open leg represents a free index.

•

Each operator or state is represented by a rectangular box labeled by its symbol (e.g.,
W_{R}
,
O
,
M
), with input and output legs corresponding to its domain and codomain.

•

A unitary
U
is represented by a black filled dot followed by a black open circle, while its adjoint
U^{\dagger}
is represented by the reverse ordering in red (red open circle followed by a red filled dot). If multiple unitaries appear (e.g.,
U
and
V
), we label them explicitly by writing the corresponding symbol (
U
,
V
, etc.) above the associated dot–circle pair.

•

The first, second, and third legs (from outermost to innermost) correspond to the Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}}
,
\mathcal{H}_{A_{2}}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{A}}
, respectively. If a fourth leg is present, the ordering is taken to be
\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}},\mathcal{H}_{A_{2}},\mathcal{H}_{\bar{A}_{2}},\mathcal{H}_{\bar{A}_{1}}
.

•

Any extra legs that are not acted on by
W_{R}
, but only attach to external states or operators, are treated as reference systems in
\mathcal{H}_{r}
and are labeled by
r
.

•

A dotted segment is not a separate wire. It is the same wire as the adjoining solid segment, drawn dotted only over the portion where the wire crosses an operator box without attaching to it. In that region the wire simply passes by the operator, so none of its indices contract with the operator. After the box, the line becomes solid again to emphasize that the wire is continuing unchanged.

•

When averaging over random unitaries, red dashed lines connect each unitary to its corresponding adjoint (e.g.,
U
to
U^{\dagger}
). These dashed connections represent the index contractions produced by the Haar integral.

Two complete example diagrams illustrating these conventions is shown below:

Example 1

\Tr\Bigg[\Tr_{\bar{A}}\Big(W_{R}U_{A_{1}}\sigma_{A_{1}}^{(0)}\chi_{A_{2}\bar{A}}\Big)\Gamma_{A_{1}}U^{\dagger}_{A_{1}}Tr_{\bar{A}}\Big(W_{R}U_{A_{1}}\sigma_{A_{1}}^{(0)}\chi_{A_{2}\bar{A}}\Big)\Gamma_{A_{1}}U^{\dagger}_{A_{1}}\Bigg]

\equiv\qquad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[width]{theory_draft_images/WGeg1.png}}}

Example 2

\operatorname{Tr}\Bigg[\operatorname{Tr}_{A_{1}\bar{A}_{1}}\left(W_{R}U_{A_{1}}V_{\bar{A}_{1}}\chi_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}}\sigma_{A_{1}\bar{A}_{2}}^{(0)}V^{\dagger}_{\bar{A}_{1}}\right)\Gamma_{A_{1}}U_{A_{1}}^{\dagger}\operatorname{Tr}_{A_{1}\bar{A}_{1}}\left(W_{R}U_{A_{1}}V_{\bar{A}_{1}}\chi_{A_{2}\bar{A}_{2}}\sigma_{A_{1}\bar{A}_{2}}^{(0)}V^{\dagger}_{\bar{A}_{1}}\right)\Gamma_{A_{1}}U_{A_{1}}^{\dagger}\Bigg]

\equiv\qquad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[width]{theory_draft_images/WGeg2.png}}}

E.3.1
Weingarten Calculus

To evaluate averages involving random unitaries, we use the Weingarten calculus, a method for computing polynomial integrals over the unitary group with respect to the Haar measure. For a unitary matrix
U\in\mathrm{U}(d)
, the expectation of a product of
n
matrix elements and their conjugates can be expressed in terms of permutations in the symmetric group
S_{n}
:

\left\langle U_{a_{1}b_{1}}\cdots U_{a_{n}b_{n}}U^{*}_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\cdots U^{*}_{\alpha_{n}\beta_{n}}\right\rangle_{U}=\sum_{\sigma,\tau\in S_{n}}\delta_{a_{1}\alpha_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots\delta_{a_{n}\alpha_{\sigma(n)}}\delta_{b_{1}\beta_{\tau(1)}}\cdots\delta_{b_{n}\beta_{\tau(n)}}\,\mathrm{Wg}(\sigma^{-1}\tau,d),

where
\mathrm{Wg}(\pi,d)
is the Weingarten function, which depends on the permutation
\pi\in S_{n}
and the dimension
d
.
For
n=2
, corresponding to the second moment,
S_{2}
has two elements: the identity
e
and the transposition
(12)
. Their Weingarten values are

\mathrm{Wg}(e,d)=\frac{1}{d^{2}-1},\qquad\mathrm{Wg}((12),d)=-\frac{1}{d(d^{2}-1)}.

(394)

Substituting these into the general formula gives

\displaystyle\left\langle U_{a_{1}b_{1}}U_{a_{2}b_{2}}U^{*}_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}U^{*}_{\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}}\right\rangle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Wg}(e,d)\big(\delta_{a_{1}\alpha_{1}}\delta_{a_{2}\alpha_{2}}\delta_{b_{1}\beta_{1}}\delta_{b_{2}\beta_{2}}+\delta_{a_{1}\alpha_{2}}\delta_{a_{2}\alpha_{1}}\delta_{b_{1}\beta_{2}}\delta_{b_{2}\beta_{1}}\big)

(395)

\displaystyle+\mathrm{Wg}((12),d)\big(\delta_{a_{1}\alpha_{2}}\delta_{a_{2}\alpha_{1}}\delta_{b_{1}\beta_{1}}\delta_{b_{2}\beta_{2}}+\delta_{a_{1}\alpha_{1}}\delta_{a_{2}\alpha_{2}}\delta_{b_{1}\beta_{2}}\delta_{b_{2}\beta_{1}}\big).

E.3.2
Diagrammatic Haar Averaging Rules

The diagrammatic procedure for performing a Haar average follows these steps
9
:

1.

Connect each black dot of a given unitary
U^{(\ell)}
to the corresponding red dot of its adjoint
\big(U^{(\ell)}\big)^{\dagger}
with a red dashed line, and likewise connect each black circle to the corresponding red circle. These red dashed lines represent the index contractions produced by the Haar average. Contractions only pair a unitary with its own adjoint (i.e.,
U^{(\ell)}
contracts only with
\big(U^{(\ell)}\big)^{\dagger}
, never with
\big(U^{(m)}\big)^{\dagger}
for
\ell\neq m
). In the present diagram there are four occurrences of the unitary/adjoint pair, yielding four admissible contraction patterns.

2.

Every closed loop composed of alternating solid lines and dotted lines is called a T-cycle.
Each T-cycle corresponds to a trace over the sequence of matrices encountered along that loop.
If a solid line is traversed in the direction opposite to its arrow, the corresponding operator appears transposed within the trace.

3.

Every closed loop composed of alternating gaps and dotted lines is called a U-cycle.
The length
c_{k}
of a U-cycle is defined as half the number of gap segments it contains.
The complete diagram gives rise to a collection of U-cycles, which together determine a coefficient

S_{c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}}
representing the weight of that particular diagram.

4.

The coefficient
S_{c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}}
can be factorized into cumulants.
For the present case with four unitaries in the diagram, only two configurations arise:
two U-cycles of length 1 each, contributing a weight

S_{1,1}=\frac{1}{d^{2}-1},

and a single U-cycle of length 2, contributing a weight

S_{2}=-\frac{1}{d(d^{2}-1)},

where
d
is the dimension of the Hilbert space on which
U\in\mathrm{SU}(d)
acts.
In our case,
d=2
.
The Haar average is obtained by summing all diagrams with their respective weights.

5.

Explicitly, the contraction identity used in the averaging procedure is

\displaystyle\left\langle U_{a_{1}b_{1}}U_{a_{2}b_{2}}U_{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}^{*}U_{\alpha_{2}\beta_{2}}^{*}\right\rangle=

\displaystyle V_{1,1}\,\delta_{a_{1}\alpha_{1}}\delta_{b_{1}\beta_{1}}\delta_{a_{2}\alpha_{2}}\delta_{b_{2}\beta_{2}}+V_{2}\,\delta_{a_{1}\alpha_{2}}\delta_{b_{1}\beta_{1}}\delta_{a_{2}\alpha_{1}}\delta_{b_{2}\beta_{2}}

(396)

\displaystyle+V_{2}\,\delta_{a_{1}\alpha_{1}}\delta_{b_{1}\beta_{2}}\delta_{a_{2}\alpha_{2}}\delta_{b_{2}\beta_{1}}+V_{1,1}\,\delta_{a_{1}\alpha_{2}}\delta_{b_{1}\beta_{2}}\delta_{a_{2}\alpha_{1}}\delta_{b_{2}\beta_{1}}.

Each term in this expression corresponds to one of the admissible contraction patterns between
U
and
U^{\dagger}
in the diagrammatic expansion.

6.

If the diagram contains several independent Haar-random unitaries
U^{(1)},U^{(2)},\ldots
, the Haar measure factorizes,

\Big\langle\,\cdots\,\Big\rangle\;=\;\int dU^{(1)}\int dU^{(2)}\cdots\,(\cdots),

so the averaging can be performed separately for each unitary label.
Equivalently, red dashed contractions only pair
U^{(\ell)}
with
\big(U^{(\ell)}\big)^{\dagger}
; there are no admissible contractions that connect
U^{(\ell)}
to
\big(U^{(m)}\big)^{\dagger}
for
\ell\neq m
.
The weight of a full contraction pattern therefore factorizes as a product of the weights obtained from each unitary’s contraction pattern.

Example:

The trace equation of the above diagram is:

\Tr\Bigg[\Tr_{\bar{A}}\Big(W_{R}U_{A_{1}}\sigma_{A_{1}}^{(0)}\chi_{A_{2}\bar{A}}\Big)\Gamma_{A_{1}}U^{\dagger}_{A_{1}}Tr_{\bar{A}}\Big(W_{R}U_{A_{1}}\sigma_{A_{1}}^{(0)}\chi_{A_{2}\bar{A}}\Big)\Gamma_{A_{1}}U^{\dagger}_{A_{1}}\Bigg]

Integration over
U
yields the 4 possible contractions:

After performing the Haar integral over
U_{A_{1}}
, the expression reduces to a sum of contraction diagrams, each weighted by its corresponding Haar coefficient:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d^{2}-1}\quad

\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[width]{theory_draft_images/WGfinal1.png}}}\quad+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d^{2}-1}\quad

\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[width]{theory_draft_images/WGfinal3.png}}}\quad-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d(d^{2}-1)}\quad

\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[width]{theory_draft_images/WGfinal2.png}}}\quad-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d(d^{2}-1)}\quad

which can also be written as a trace equation:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d^{2}-1}\Bigg[\Tr\Big(\Gamma_{A_{1}}\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}\Big)^{2}\Tr\Big[\Tr_{\bar{A}}\Big(W_{R}\chi_{A_{2}\bar{A}}\Big)^{2}\Big]+\Tr\Big[\Big(\Gamma_{A_{1}}\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}\Big)^{2}\Big]\Tr\Big[\Tr_{A_{1}\bar{A}}\Big(W_{R}\chi_{A_{2}\bar{A}}\Big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{d}\Tr\Big(\Gamma_{A_{1}}\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}\Big)^{2}\Tr_{A_{1}\bar{A}}\Big(W_{R}\chi_{A_{2}\bar{A}}\Big)^{2}\Big]-\frac{1}{d}\Tr\Big[\Big(\Gamma_{A_{1}}\sigma^{(0)}_{A_{1}}\Big)^{2}\Big]\Tr\Big[\Tr_{\bar{A}}\Big(W_{R}\chi_{A_{2}\bar{A}}\Big)^{2}\Big]\Bigg]

(397)

For our calculations, we explicitly performed all computations using the
RTNI
package
30
, available at
https://github.com/MotohisaFukuda/RTNI
.

Appendix F
Matter-geometry entanglement is needed for gravity

One can see why states in (
6
) are needed from the perspective of perturbative quantum gravity.
Suppose a matter field
\phi
is coupled to gravitons
h_{\mu\nu}
on a fixed background
g_{\mu\nu}
. For any coherent state of the matter, we can work in semi-classical gravity and obtain the solution for the metric perturbation, which can be written as some coherent state of gravitons. Let’s denote such states as
|\phi_{i}\rangle|h_{i}\rangle
for each solution of the linearized Einstein’s equation where
|\phi_{i}\rangle,|h_{i}\rangle
denote the states of the matter field and gravitons respectively, and
i
is an abstract index that labels the different solutions with energy below some cutoff so that linearized gravity remains valid. These states, which are generally overcomplete, span a low energy subspace
\mathcal{H}_{L}=span\{|\phi_{i}\rangle|h_{i}\rangle\}
of the effective field theory.
Suppose a background geometry
g_{\mu\nu}
emerges from the entanglement structure of a code
\mathcal{C}\cong\mathcal{H}_{L}
with subsystem complementary recovery,
the logical degrees of freedom are now used to simulate a theory of the matter field coupled to gravitons on a fixed background
g_{\mu\nu}
. Even though the Hilbert space of a gauge theory is not factorizable due to non-trivial gauge constraints imposed by perturbative quantum gravity, it is embedable as a subspace of a factorizable Hilbert space by including the edge modes
8
8

8

In quantum reference frame language, we take
\mathcal{H}_{L}
to be isomorphic to the kinematic Hilbert space of the gauge theory coupled to matter. When the gauge constraints are imposed, the theory lives in a gauge invariant, or physical subspace. However, to avoid confusing “physical” Hilbert space in QRF with that in QEC, we will work directly with the kinematic Hilbert space. More precisely, if we think of
|\phi_{i}\rangle|h_{i}\rangle
as states spanning the physical gauge invariant subspace, then we need to first embed this space into
\mathcal{H}_{L}
of a subsystem code that satisfies complementary recovery.
.

As the code supports subsystem complementary recovery on its logical qubits, we can write the encoded state by starting with the logical state, a shared entangled resource
|\chi\rangle
, and unitaries
U_{A}\otimes U_{\bar{A}}
such that

|\tilde{\psi}\rangle=U_{A}\otimes U_{\bar{A}}\Big(\sum_{i}c_{i}|\phi_{i}\rangle|h_{i}\rangle|\chi\rangle\Big).

(398)

Instead of treating both
|\phi_{i}\rangle,|h_{i}\rangle
as matter fields, we can now absorb
|h_{i}\rangle
into the spacetime degrees of freedom, i.e.,
|h_{i}\rangle|\chi\rangle\rightarrow|\chi_{i}\rangle
. Hence, we recover codewords of the form in eqn (
6
) and such a codeword then permits entanglement between matter and geometry, as it should be due to gravity. The above re-identification can be understood as defining a new code which encode
span\{|\phi_{i}\rangle\}
into a smaller isomorphic subspace
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{H}
. This corresponds to code concatenation where matter field
\phi
is first encoded in the combined (kinematical) Hilbert space
\mathcal{C}
of
\phi
coupled to gravitons (outer code), which is then encoded in a subsystem code satisfying complementary recovery (inner code). .

One can also relate such code concatenation with a heuristic picture of renormalization group, where the flow from UV to IR corresponds to the sequence of Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
as we progressively “integrate out” the UV degrees of freedom associated with the higher energy physics from which the background spacetime geometry and the graviton degrees of freedom emerges. For this reason,
13
also identifies entropy
S(\chi)
with the UV/spacetime degrees of freedom whereas
S(\sigma_{a})
with that of the IR/matter field degrees of freedom.

Appendix G
Monotonic RT Lengths in AdS
3
with Backreaction

Here we provide an explicit example where the area of the (classical) extremal surface does increase with bulk entropy in the presence of gravity. We construct a static, circularly symmetric solution of the
Einstein equations in
2+1
dimensions with negative cosmological constant
sourced by a static anisotropic fluid
38
;
10
;
11
, then compute the length of the
Ryu–Takayanagi (RT) geodesic anchored on a boundary interval.
We work in Schwarzschild-like coordinates
(t,r,\theta)
and assume a static,
rotationally symmetric metric of the form

ds^{2}=-A(r)^{2}\,dt^{2}+B(r)^{2}\,dr^{2}+r^{2}\,d\theta^{2},

(399)

We now consider the two-parameter family

A(r)^{2}=1+\beta r^{2},\qquad B(r)^{2}=\frac{1}{1+\alpha r^{2}},

(400)

with constants
\alpha>0
and
\beta
. The matter sector is taken to be an anisotropic fluid with energy density
\mu(r)
, radial pressure
p_{r}(r)
, and tangential pressure
p_{\theta}(r)
. In the coordinate basis
(t,r,\theta)
, the stress-energy tensor is

T_{\mu\nu}=(\mu+p_{\theta})\,u_{\mu}u_{\nu}+p_{\theta}\,g_{\mu\nu}+(p_{r}-p_{\theta})\,\chi_{\mu}\chi_{\nu},

(401)

where
u^{\mu}
is the fluid velocity, normalized by
u_{\mu}u^{\mu}=-1
, and
\chi^{\mu}
is a unit spacelike vector in the radial direction, satisfying
\chi_{\mu}\chi^{\mu}=1
and
u_{\mu}\chi^{\mu}=0
.
The fluid velocity and the unit spacelike vector in the radial direction are

u^{\mu}=\left(\frac{1}{A(r)},\,0,\,0\right),\quad u_{\mu}=\bigl(-A(r),\,0,\,0\bigr),\qquad\chi^{\mu}=\left(0,\,\frac{1}{B(r)},\,0\right),\quad\chi_{\mu}=\bigl(0,\,B(r),\,0\bigr).

Hence, the nonzero components of the stress-energy tensor are

T_{tt}=\mu(r)\,A(r)^{2},\qquad T_{rr}=p_{r}(r)\,B(r)^{2},\qquad T_{\theta\theta}=p_{\theta}(r)\,r^{2}.

(402)

The Einstein equations with cosmological constant are

G_{\mu\nu}+\Lambda g_{\mu\nu}=\kappa T_{\mu\nu},

(403)

where
\kappa=8\pi G_{3}
is the three-dimensional gravitational coupling.
Substituting the metric ansatz and the anisotropic stress tensor into the Einstein equations, we obtain three independent equations:

\displaystyle\frac{B^{\prime}(r)}{r\,B(r)^{3}}-\Lambda=\kappa\,\mu(r),

(404)

\displaystyle\frac{A^{\prime}(r)}{r\,A(r)}+\Lambda B(r)^{2}=\kappa\,p_{r}(r)\,B(r)^{2},

(405)

\displaystyle\frac{B(r)A^{\prime\prime}(r)-A^{\prime}(r)B^{\prime}(r)}{A(r)B(r)^{3}}+\Lambda=\kappa\,p_{\theta}(r).

(406)

Solving these equations give:

\displaystyle\mu(r)

\displaystyle=\mu\equiv\frac{-\,(\alpha+\Lambda)}{\kappa},

(407)

\displaystyle p_{r}(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\kappa}\left[\Lambda+\frac{\beta(1+\alpha r^{2})}{1+\beta r^{2}}\right],

(408)

\displaystyle p_{\theta}(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\kappa}\left[\Lambda+\frac{\beta\bigl(1+2\alpha r^{2}+\alpha\beta r^{4}\bigr)}{(1+\beta r^{2})^{2}}\right].

(409)

Thus the metric is supported by an anisotropic fluid with constant energy density
\mu
. The vacuum AdS
3
limit is recovered by setting
\mu=p_{r}=p_{\theta}=0
,
which implies
\alpha=\beta=-\Lambda
. The density is positive provided
-\Lambda>\alpha>0
.
Moreover, the source is genuinely anisotropic unless
\beta=\alpha
. In the special case
\beta=\alpha
, one finds

p_{r}(r)=p_{\theta}(r)=\frac{\Lambda+\alpha}{\kappa}=-\mu,

(410)

so the stress tensor becomes isotropic and behaves like a vacuum-energy source rather than a generic matter fluid. In what follows, we therefore restrict to the anisotropic branch
\beta\neq\alpha
.

The conservation equation
\nabla_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}=0
reduces to

p_{r}^{\prime}(r)+(\mu+p_{r})\frac{A^{\prime}(r)}{A(r)}+\frac{p_{r}-p_{\theta}}{r}=0,

(411)

and one verifies by direct substitution of (
407
)–(
409
) that it is satisfied identically.

For subsequent computations, it is convenient to rewrite the radial
coefficient in terms of

\alpha(\mu)\equiv-\bigl(\Lambda+\kappa\mu\bigr),

(412)

Throughout, we restrict to this range so that
\alpha(\mu)>0
and the geometry remains AdS-like rather than transitioning to a different asymptotic spacetime. Hence on a constant time slice the induced spatial metric is

dl^{2}\equiv ds^{2}\big|_{dt=0}=B(r)^{2}\,dr^{2}+r^{2}\,d\theta^{2}=\frac{dr^{2}}{1+\alpha r^{2}}+r^{2}\,d\theta^{2}.

(413)

A spacelike curve anchored on the boundary interval can be
written as
r(\theta)
, and its length is

\mathcal{L}[r(\theta)]=\int d\theta\,\mathcal{L}_{\text{geo}}(r,r^{\prime}),\qquad\mathcal{L}_{\text{geo}}(r,r^{\prime})=\sqrt{\frac{{r^{\prime}}^{2}}{1+\alpha r^{2}}+r^{2}},

(414)

where

r^{\prime}\equiv\frac{dr}{d\theta}.

(415)

We view
\theta
as the “time” variable for this one-dimensional
mechanical problem. The Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{\text{geo}}
depends on
r

and
r^{\prime}
, but not explicitly on
\theta
, so there is a conserved Hamiltonian
(energy)

\mathcal{H}=-\frac{r^{2}}{\mathcal{L}_{\text{geo}}}.

(416)

To identify this constant, we use the turning point. We consider a geodesic
symmetric about
\theta=0
, with minimal radius
r=r_{\ast}
at
\theta=0
.
At the turning point
r=r_{\ast}
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{geo}}(r_{\ast},0)=r_{\ast},\qquad\mathcal{H}=-\,\frac{r_{\ast}^{2}}{\mathcal{L}_{\text{geo}}(r_{\ast},0)}=-\,r_{\ast}.

(417)

Now, Eq. (
416
) can be rewritten as the first-order constraint

\mathcal{L}_{\text{geo}}(r,r^{\prime})=\frac{r^{2}}{r_{\ast}}.

(418)

This first-order Hamiltonian equation determines the geodesic profile

r(\theta)
and directly implies

\frac{d\theta}{dr}=\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}=\frac{r_{\ast}}{r\,\sqrt{1+\alpha r^{2}}\,\sqrt{r^{2}-r_{\ast}^{2}}},

(419)

We consider a geodesic symmetric about
\theta=0
, with endpoints at

\theta=\pm\Delta\theta/2
on the boundary. The half opening angle is

\frac{\Delta\theta}{2}=\int_{r_{\ast}}^{\infty}\frac{d\theta}{dr}\,dr=\int_{r_{\ast}}^{\infty}\frac{r_{\ast}\,dr}{r\,\sqrt{1+\alpha r^{2}}\,\sqrt{r^{2}-r_{\ast}^{2}}}.

(420)

This integral can be evaluated exactly. One finds

\int_{r_{\ast}}^{\infty}\frac{r_{\ast}\,dr}{r\,\sqrt{1+\alpha r^{2}}\,\sqrt{r^{2}-r_{\ast}^{2}}}=\tan^{-1}\!\left(\frac{1}{r_{\ast}\sqrt{\alpha}}\right),

(421)

so that

\frac{\Delta\theta}{2}=\tan^{-1}\!\left(\frac{1}{r_{\ast}\sqrt{\alpha}}\right)

(422)

and hence

\tan\!\left(\frac{\Delta\theta}{2}\right)=\frac{1}{r_{\ast}\sqrt{\alpha}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad r_{\ast}(\Delta\theta,\alpha)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}\,\tan(\Delta\theta/2)}.

(423)

The Hamiltonian relation Eq. (
418
),
allows us to compute the RT length directly:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RT}}=\int_{-\Delta\theta/2}^{\Delta\theta/2}\mathcal{L}_{\text{geo}}\,d\theta=2\int_{0}^{\Delta\theta/2}\frac{r(\theta)^{2}}{r_{\ast}}\,d\theta=2\int_{r_{\ast}}^{r_{\max}}\frac{r\,dr}{\sqrt{1+\alpha r^{2}}\,\sqrt{r^{2}-r_{\ast}^{2}}}.

(424)

This integral can be evaluated in closed form. The result is

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RT}}\!\left(r_{\ast},r_{\max},\alpha\right)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\,\sinh^{-1}\!\left(\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}\,\sqrt{r_{\max}^{2}-r_{\ast}^{2}}}{\sqrt{1+\alpha r_{\ast}^{2}}}\right).

(425)

Now, we fix the boundary interval
\Delta\theta
and the UV cutoff
r_{\max}
and
ask how
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RT}}
changes as we vary
\mu
.
In the UV regime
r_{\max}\gg r_{\ast}
, we may approximate

\sqrt{r_{\max}^{2}-r_{\ast}^{2}}\simeq r_{\max},

(426)

Substituting
r_{\ast}
, the RT length reduces to

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RT}}(\alpha)\simeq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\,\sinh^{-1}\!\bigl(C\sqrt{\alpha}\bigr),\qquad C\equiv r_{\max}\sin(\Delta\theta/2)>0.

(427)

To study the
\mu
–dependence, it is enough to analyze
\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RT}}/\partial\alpha

for the approximate expression (
427
).

Defining
\mathcal{X}\equiv C\sqrt{\alpha}\geq 0
, a short calculation gives

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RT}}}{\partial\alpha}=\alpha^{-3/2}\left[\frac{\mathcal{X}}{\sqrt{1+\mathcal{X}^{2}}}-\sinh^{-1}\mathcal{X}\right].

(428)

Since
\alpha>0
, the sign is controlled by the bracket. Consider

\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})\equiv\sinh^{-1}\mathcal{X}-\frac{\mathcal{X}}{\sqrt{1+\mathcal{X}^{2}}}

(429)

We have
\mathcal{F}(0)=0
, and

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(\mathcal{X})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\mathcal{X}^{2}}}-\frac{1}{(1+\mathcal{X}^{2})^{3/2}}=\frac{\mathcal{X}^{2}}{(1+\mathcal{X}^{2})^{3/2}}\geq 0,

(430)

so
\mathcal{F}
is monotonically increasing and

\sinh^{-1}\mathcal{X}\;\geq\;\frac{\mathcal{X}}{\sqrt{1+\mathcal{X}^{2}}}\quad\text{for all }\mathcal{X}\geq 0.

(431)

It follows that

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RT}}}{\partial\alpha}\leq 0.

(432)

Finally, since

\alpha(\mu)=-(\Lambda+\kappa\mu),\qquad\frac{d\alpha}{d\mu}=-\kappa<0,

(433)

we have

\frac{d\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RT}}}{d\mu}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RT}}}{\partial\alpha}\,\frac{d\alpha}{d\mu}=-\kappa\,\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RT}}}{\partial\alpha}\;\geq\;0.

(434)

Thus, at fixed opening angle
\Delta\theta
and in the UV limit
r_{\max}\gg r_{\ast}
,
the RT geodesic length
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{RT}}
is a monotonically increasing
function of
\mu
.

It then follows from the first law of thermodynamics
33
;
6
that we can rewrite the (comoving) entropy density in terms of the energy density and pressure contributions. For simplicity, we restrict ourselves to a family of the static solutions such that the anisotropic pressure profile is fixed when
\mu
is varied. This then yields that
\partial s/\partial\mu=1/T
. Therefore, the spacelike geodesic length increases as a function of (thermal) entropy.
